### Frequency and ω
    1) Do `ω` and `f` have the same units?
        - Yes.
        - `f` (frequency): Hz = s⁻¹ (cycles per second)
        - `ω` (angular frequency): rad·s⁻¹
        - Since radians are dimensionless:
        - `[ω] = [f] = s⁻¹`
        - Difference:
            - `f` counts cycles
            - `ω` measures angular phase (in radians)
        - Relation: `ω = 2πf`


    2) Is “per second” (s⁻¹) really a unit?
        - Yes.
        - s⁻¹ is a valid SI unit meaning “per second”
        - It represents a `rate`
        - Why it seems odd:
            - You expect “[something] per second”
            - But that “something” (e.g. cycles) is just
              a `count` → dimensionless
        - So: - `cycles/s → s⁻¹`

    - Key idea:
        - Frequency is a rate of occurrence
        - The “thing occurring” has no physical dimension

    - Final:
        - `s⁻¹` is a complete unit
        - “per second” is just its English description



### Our World is 3D
    - https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46690295
    > we perceive as Dark Energy in our 4D world
    - This is a bit of a technicality, but we don't live in a 4D
      world, we live in a `3+1D` world; the 3 spatial dimensions
      are interchangeable, but the 1 time-related dimension is
      not interchangeable with the other three
        - (the _metric is not commutative_).
    - I'm bringing this up because many people seem to think
      space and time are completely unified in modern physics,
      and this is very much not the case.

### Bergson and simultaneity (important)
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/metaphysics/Bergson.txt
    - 2. _Simultaneity_
        - A central tenet of SR is that simultaneity is relative:
            - simultaneous events in one reference frame may not
              be simultaneous in another.
        - Bergson challenged this, asserting that an `absolute
          simultaneity` underlies the relative simultaneity
          described by Einstein.
        - He believed SR conflated the mathematical model of time
          with the actual experience of time.
    - Bergson's criticisms of SR involved:
        - The distinction between scientific and lived time.
        - The rejection of relative simultaneity in favor of an
          _underlying absolute simultaneity_.
        - The belief that time cannot be fully understood through
          the `SPATIALIZED` framework of physics.
        - The need to ground physical theories in the concrete
          experience of time.

### Photon spin
    - (Never knew that photon spin really appears as
      polarisation.)
    - And photons are no ordinary particles.
        - For one thing, they have no `mass`.
        - They have `spin` (intrinsic angular momentum) which we
          perceive as polarisation.
        - They also have something called `phase`, which means
          that these particles can also behave like waves.

### Two deep laws
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/courses/physics-for-poets.md
    - His falling-body law (Chapter 1) is actually a by-product
      of this research. Galileo used two important physical
      principles which he did not invent:
        - _Inertia_:
            - An object moving on a level surface will continue
              to move in the same direction (in a straight
              line?!) at constant speed unless disturbed.
        - _Superposition_:
            - If an object is subjected to two separate
              influences, each producing a characteristic type of
              motion, it responds to each _without modifying_ its
              response to the other.

### Emergence (Sean Carroll)
    - https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.15468
    - Everyone agrees that emergence is important, but they don’t
      agree what the word means. When a system 'is' a _composite_
      of many parts, there may be NOVEL properties or behaviors
      of the composite system that aren't easily predictable from
      the properties and behaviors of the parts themselves.
    - Emergence is relevant when there's both
        - a “micro” description
            - (more fundamental, comprehensive, lower-level)
        - and
        - a “macro” description
            - (emergent, coarse-grained, higher-level) of the
              same system...
        - both of which capture important properties.
    - Emergence is ubiquitous, and is crucial for dealing with
      reality: we are able to model and predict features of the
      world with dramatically _incomplete information_ about it.
    - We can talk about tables and chairs, not to mention people,
      without anything close to a complete picture of the
      elementary particles and forces from which they are
      apparently made.
    - Properties like “temperature" or “wetness” or
      “irritability" do not apply to individual particles, but
      are useful emergent features of the macroscopic world. Even
      more abstract notions like chance and `probability` may be
      thought of as emergent phenomena.

### Inertial motion is gravitational pull
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/CM/Jakob-CM.txt
    - Historically physicists used two terms
        - "gravitational mass" and
        - "intertial mass"
            - (they thought there were two types because of the
              different roles of `mass`)
    - But the idea that gravitational mass and interial mass are
      the same thing (now simply called 'mass') was a key insight
      leading Einstein to GR.
    - From this perspective, the resistance of an object to being
      moved around (inertial mass) is a result of the
      GRAVITATIONAL PULL of all the objects in the universe.
        - This is 'Machs Principle'

        - the orthogonal [^red] lines are the rays of geometrical

        - the `green` lines denote constant values of action

        - the [^red] lines are the orthogonal object
          trajectories.


### Action
    - Schrödinger equation emerges mathematically from classical
      mechanics (2012)
        - $MY_NOTES/physics/CM/Schrodinger-eqn-from-CM.txt
    - Interesting: "Before Hamilton, "action" meant "accumulated
      living force", i.e. the integral of the kinetic energy."
    - The principle of the minimum action of Maupertuis was true
      only in some restricted cases, and it was false in most
      mechanics problems.
    - Hamilton has introduced a new physical quantity, for which
      he has used only the name `function S`.
    - He formulated a variational principle for the `function S`,
      analogous to the principle of the minimum action, but from
      which it is possible to deduce the system of equations of
      Lagrange, so it has general applicability.
    - Hamilton's `function S` is relativistically invariant and
      nowadays it is usually called Hamilton's action. It is the
      integral of the Lagrangian, not of the kinetic energy, like
      the traditional action.
    - It is proportional with the phase of the wave function in
      QM. In relativistic mechanics, the Lagrangian is the
      component of the momentum-energy that is tangent to the
      trajectory in space-time, so `function S` is the line
      integral of the momentum-energy over the trajectory in
      space-time.
    - So Hamilton's variational principle is quite different in
      meaning and applicability from the 'principle of the
      minimum action' that existed before him. It remains true in
      all forms of physics that have been discovered after
      Hamilton.

### Pitch versus Frequency
    - Pitch and frequency are related, but not exactly the same.

        - Frequency is the number of waves that pass a given
          point per second, measured in hertz (Hz). It's
          a physical quantity; precisely measured.

        - Pitch is a _perceptual property_ of sounds that allows
          them to be ordered on a musical scale.
        - It's how humans perceive the frequency of a sound.
          Higher frequency sounds are perceived as having
          a higher pitch, and lower frequency sounds are
          perceived as having a lower pitch.

    - Doppler effect:
        - This describes the change in frequency (and
          consequently pitch) of a wave in relation to an
          observer who is moving relative to the wave source.
        - As a sound source approaches an observer, the frequency
          appears higher, and as it moves away, the frequency
          appears lower. This change in frequency is _perceived_
          as a change in pitch.

    - So, while frequency is a measurable attribute of waves,
      pitch is the human perception of that frequency.

### Moment / momentum
    - see also: $MY_NOTES/physics/CM/moment-torque-inertia.txt
    - moment (noun): from Latin momentum, shortened from the
      latin movimentum "a movement, a motion," from the verb
      movere "to move."
        - The Indo-European root is meu - "to push away."
        - Related borrowings from Latin include motor and mob (a
          group of people moving recklessly).
    - The Latin noun `momentum` came to refer to a weight or
      object just big enough to cause _movement in a balance
      scale_, hence "a small amount";
        - that sense of 'moment' is still seen in physics.
    - In nontechnical English, the word `moment` came to refer to
      a small movement of TIME rather than MASS, hence the
      current meanings "a brief period of time, an instant."

### Energy not invariant, but 'Action' is
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/thermo/Energy-Jennifer.txt
    - ... the complete answer is that energy is NOT AN INVARIANT
      quantity.
    - Yes, it is conserved in an isolated system and yes, as
      potential energy, it usually does remain unchanged between
      reference frames (because potential energy most commonly
      refers to quantities defined within a system, howsoever
      that system is viewed).
    - But, most generally, energy DOES VARY between frames of
      reference, and it is the total _ACTION_ between definite
      end-states that is the true invariant quantity.

### Potentials are REAL?
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/CM/Jakob-CM.txt
    - NOTE: we can’t measure the 'potential' or 'potential
      energy' of an object directly. We can only measure the
      resulting FORCE
        - `F = − ∂V(x) ∂x`.
    - This follows because we could add an arbitrary constant
      `V(x) → ˜V(x) = V(x) + c` without changing the resulting
      force.
    - This means we can shift the potential however we want. In
      geometrical terms, we can say that the force remains
      unchanged because the slope remains unchanged by constant
      shifts of the potential.
        - $MY_IMAGES/physics/potential-not-directly-measurable.jpg
    - Only the DIFFERENCE between the `PE` of an object at one
      position and its `PE` at some other _reference position_
      are physically important.
        - We usually shift the potential so any object at the
          equilibrium position has zero `PE` and then specify the
          `PE` at other positions relative to this position.
    - Aside:
        - Historically, physicists used this observation to
          conclude potentials are only convenient calculation
          devices that AREN’T REAL because we can’t measure them
          directly.
        - Working with a potential is much simpler than with
          forces, because a force is described by a vector
          function `F(x)` while a potential is an ordinary
          function (scalar?) `φ(x)`.
            - Think my OU tutor said this in 2008?
        - Moreover, the freedom to shift the potential often
          allows calculations to be simplified.
        - BUT ...
            - from a modern perspective, potentials ARE more than
              just mathematical tools.
            - E.g., the Aharonov-Bohm experiment showed that
              PARTICLES CAN BE AFFECTED in regions where ONLY the
              potential is non-zero and everthing else is zero.
                - alternative wording: PARTICLES CAN BE
                  INFLUENCED in areas where a potential is
                  present, even if NO FORCE is acting on them.
            - In other words, it has been experimentally verified
              that potentials are REAL.
            - In some sense, potentials are at the heart of
              modern physics and if you want to learn more about
              them, see: Jakob Physics from Finance
                - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/Undergrad/Jakob-Schwichtenberg-Finance-Guage-Theory.pdf

    - Never appreciated what semiconductors are useful for:
        - While electrical conductors are constructed from metals
          such as copper or aluminum, and many materials are
          insulators which do not conduct current at all, there
          is a group of materials such as silicon or gallium
          whose electrical conductivity can be controlled
          externally with an electrical _potential_.
        - Therefore, these substances can serve as _switches_ and
          _memory storage_ for information.
    - Ah yes, I always associate light with heat, but not
      neccessarily so ...
        - The light sources we were familiar with before 1900,
          the Sun, candles, oil lamps, light bulbs, etc., were
          _hot bodies emitting heat_ radiation and light.
        - The color we see corresponds to the temperature of the
          radiating body. In the case of the Sun, the yellow
          coloration corresponds to a surface temperature of
          6,000 degrees Celsius, and a light wavelength of about
          500 nanometers. But sunlight also contains other
          _wavelengths_ (i.e., colors)

### Acoustical Radiation Force ARF
    - When sound WAVES travel through a medium like air or water,
      they carry energy with them. This energy can exert a force
      on objects in their path.
    - Acoustical Radiation Force refers to the force exerted by
      sound waves on an object when they strike it.
    - Imagine you have a floating ball in water, and you generate
      intense sound waves near it. These sound waves will carry
      energy, and some of that energy will be transferred to the
      ball. As a result, the ball will experience a force pushing
      it in the direction of the sound waves.
    - This phenomenon is essential in various applications,
      especially medical fields. One example is ultrasound
      imaging. In ultrasound, sound waves are sent into the body,
      and the echoes produced by the internal structures are used
      to create images.
        - Additionally, the ARF generated by the sound waves can
          also be used to move or manipulate tiny objects inside
          the body, such as drug-carrying microbubbles or cells.
    - ARF is the force exerted by sound waves on objects, and it
      has practical applications in medical imaging.

    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/QM/QFT/Particle-code-moffat.pdf

      strength of the gravitational force, is NOT A DIMENSIONLESS

      any QG calculations as meaningless infinite quantities.

### Relationship: Maths and Physics
    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/Maths/Math-Phy-General/Sadri_hassani_mathematical_physics_02.pdf
    - The quotation by Weyl is a perfect description of the
      natural intimacy between the work of mathematicians and
      physicists, and the unnatural estrangement between the two
      camps:
        - “In this drama of mathematics and physics—which
          fertilize each other in the dark, but which prefer to
          deny and misconstrue each other face to face—I cannot,
          however, resist playing the role of a messenger,
          albeit, as I have abundantly learned, often an
          unwelcome one,”
    - Some of the most beautiful mathematics has been motivated
      by physics (differential equations by Newtonian mechanics,
      differential geometry by GR, and _operator theory_ by QM).
    - And some of the most fundamental physics has been expressed
      in the most beautiful poetry of mathematics (mechanics in
      symplectic geometry, and fundamental forces in Lie group
      theory).
    - Also Sadri:
        - It is said that mathematics is the language of Nature.
          If so, physics is its poetry.
        - Nature started to whisper into our ears when Egyptians
          and Babylonians were compelled to invent and use
          mathematics in their daily activities.
        - The faint geometric and arithmetical pidgin of over
          four thousand years ago, suitable for rudimentary
          conversations with nature as applied to simple
          landscaping, has turned into a sophisticated language
          in which the heart of matter is articulated.

### Force: mystic concept really
    - Chomsky https://chomsky.info/201401__/
    - Friedrich Lange, in his 19th century history of
      `materialism`, observed we have:
        - “so accustomed ourselves to the abstract notion of
          forces, or rather to a notion hovering in a mystic
          obscurity between _abstraction and concrete
          comprehension_, that we no longer find any difficulty
          in making one particle of matter act upon another
          without immediate contact, through void space without
          any material link.
    - From such ideas the great mathematicians and physicists of
      the 17th century were far removed. They were all genuine
      Materialists in the sense of ancient Materialism that they
      considered `immediate contact` a condition of influence.”
    - This transition over time is “one of the most important
      turning-points in the history of Materialism, depriving the
      doctrine of much significance, if any at all."
    - “What Newton held to be a great absurdity (no philosophic
      thinker could think it), is prized by posterity as Newton’s
      great discovery of the harmony of the universe!”
    - Similar conclusions are common in the history of science.
      In the 20th century, Alexander Koyré observed that Newton
      demonstrated “a purely materialistic pattern of nature is
      utterly impossible (and a purely materialistic or
      mechanistic physics, such as that of Lucretius or of
      Descartes, is utterly impossible, too)”;
        - His mathematical physics required the “admission into
          the body of science of incomprehensible and
          inexplicable 'facts’ imposed up on us by empiricism,”
          by what is observed and our conclusions from these
          observations.


### General theory of motion(!)
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/spacetime/einstein_companion.txt
    - Michel Janssen tells the story of Einstein's failed quest
      for a _general relativity of MOTION_.
        - However as a new theory about gravity and space-time,
          GR was a triumphant success.
    - However, General covariance, should NOT BE conflated with
      a general relativity of motion, as Einstein eventually
      conceded (Kretschmann 1917, Einstein 1918e).
    - But Janssen also argues that Einstein's theory does
      implement a related principle called the "relativity of the
      gravitational field" (CPAE 7, Doc. 31, [p. 20]). 32
    - ALSO: What this told Einstein was: any satisfactory theory
      of gravity should make _gravity_ and _inertia_ two sides of
      the same coin. (Stachel said the same in the 'concepts of
      QG' book)
        - So 3 key concepts?
            - Gravity
            - Inertia
            - Acceleration (local equivalence of gravity and
              acceleration)

### Newton
    - I recently read 'Never at Rest', and key things stand out
      about his accomplishments:
        - Deep scholarship:
            - Learn from your predecessors as much as possible;
              pick up their techniques, at the same time be
              sceptical, do not believe much, even when you're
              learning/mastering techniques.
            - Eg: Newton mastered Descartes early in his career,
              used that mathematics to open up Calculus, but at
              later stage became disillusioned with the analytic
              method, and converted his thought to pure geometric
              methods.

        - Imitate the masters before you:
            - Newton was one of the deepest students of the
              ancients. He'd ferret out obscure passages, and use
              them to solve his problems.
            - Eg: He knew the intricacies of the analysis
              / synthesis method from Pappus, which most people
              barely knew.

        - Dedication to truth:
            - desiring certainty and taking nothing less.
              Insanely high standards.

### Waves: Energy / Power
    - The energy of the wave depends on both the `amplitude` and
      the `frequency`.
    - If the energy of each wavelength is considered to be
      a discrete packet of energy, a high-frequency wave will
      deliver more of these packets per unit time than
      a low-frequency wave.
    - note: the average rate of energy transfer in mechanical
      waves is proportional to both the _square_ of the amplitude
      and the _square_ of the frequency.
        - If two mechanical waves have equal amplitudes, but one
          wave has a frequency equal to twice the frequency of
          the other, the higher-frequency wave will have a rate
          of energy transfer a factor of four times as great as
          the rate of energy transfer of the lower-frequency
          wave.
    - Note: although the rate of energy transport is proportional
      to both the square of the amplitude and square of the
      frequency in _mechanical waves_, the rate of energy
      transfer in EM waves is proportional to the square of the
      amplitude, but INDEPENDENT of the frequency.
    - more info: Search "Difference: Mechanical Waves versus EM waves"
        - $MY_NOTES/physics/EM/EM-tips.txt

    Another difference between mechanical waves and EM:
        - The photoelectric effect cannot be adequately explained
          by classical electromagnetic (EM) wave theory. The
          discovery of this phenomenon and its explanation by
          Einstein played a crucial role in the development of
          quantum mechanics. Here’s why classical theory fails
          and quantum theory succeeds in explaining the
          photoelectric effect:
    - Classical EM Wave Theory and the Photoelectric Effect
        - Classical EM theory treats light as a continuous wave,
          and according to this theory, the energy of the wave
          should depend on the intensity (amplitude squared) of
          the light. If classical theory were correct in
          explaining the photoelectric effect, the following
          should be observed:
        - Intensity Dependence: Increasing the intensity of light
          should increase the energy imparted to the electrons.
          Therefore, a sufficiently intense light, regardless of
          its frequency, should eventually provide enough energy
          to eject electrons from the metal.
        - Time Delay: Electrons should be ejected after some time
          delay, as they gradually absorb enough energy from the
          continuous wave to overcome the work function of the
          metal.
    - Observations from the Photoelectric Effect
        - However, the experimental observations of the
          photoelectric effect show the following:
        - Frequency Threshold: Electrons are ejected from the
          metal only if the frequency of the incident light is
          above a certain threshold, regardless of the intensity.
          Light with a frequency below this threshold, no matter
          how intense, does not eject any electrons.
        - Immediate Ejection: When the frequency of the light is
          above the threshold, electrons are ejected almost
          instantaneously, with no observable time delay, even at
          very low intensities.
        - Kinetic Energy Dependence: The kinetic energy of the
          ejected electrons depends on the frequency of the
          incident light and not on its intensity. Higher
          frequency light (above the threshold) results in
          electrons with higher kinetic energy.

### Physics humour
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/EM/Heaviside-maverick.txt
    - The abbreviations `T` and `T′` had their origin in
      a three-way correspondence by the three Scottish physicists
      Thomson, P.G. Tait and Maxwell.
    - They wrote on postcards and to conserve space they
      abbreviated wherever they could.
    - Thomson was `T`, Tait was `T′` and Maxwell was `dp/dt`
      (from the equation `dp/dt = JCM` in Tait’s book on
      thermodynamics).
    - In their code, Hermann Helmholtz was `H^2` and John Tyndall
      was `T''`.
    - Tait did not have a high opinion of Tyndall and, in his
      cutting way, used to say that `T''` indicated
      a second-order quantity.

### Wave nature of matter

    - ... Geometers call this kind of structure a _FIBER BUNDLE_,
      each dial representing a fibre, and the entire set of them
      over all space-time points comprising the bundle.
    - What physicists call the vector potential, mathematicians
      call the connection over the bundle.
    - The geometry of our world, ala Weyl–Schrödinger, is
      therefore of a 1D fibre bundle over the 4D manifold of
      space-time.
    - Electromagnetic forces and gravity are “explained” as
      distortions in this geometry associated with the
      distribution of energy, mass, and electricity throughout
      the universe.

### Boltzmann Equation
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/thermo/Julian-History-Thermo.txt
    - The task was then to show that whatever form the energy
      distribution function might have at some initial time it
      would necessarily evolve eventually to the Maxwell
      distribution and thereafter remain unchanged. Of course,
      this applied to the average macroscopic distribution
      function. At the microscopic level, small fluctuations
      could always happen.
    - Boltzmann was able to find an equation that should govern
      the equilibration process and would indeed, whatever the
      initial distribution, lead to the Maxwell distribution. The
      equation soon became known as the Boltzmann equation and
      proved its utility and viability in many calculations
      involving countless different processes.
    - It is one of the most _important equations in physics_.
    - For example, cast in a form that takes into account quantum
      effects, it plays a hugely important role in the
      description of processes that take place in the early
      universe though, rather remarkably, it is used in that
      context to describe how the medium that permeates the
      universe comes out of equilibirum.
    - ... the important thing about the Boltzmann equation is
      that, by its very construction, it has an inbuilt tendency
      to _reduce deviations_ from the Maxwell distribution.
    - Unlike the _time-reversal symmetric_ equations taken to
      describe the collisions of microscopic particles,
      Boltzmann’s equation is _time-asymmetric_.
        - (There was nothing wrong with the theory of individual
          collisions. The problem lay in an implicit assumption,
          made by both Maxwell and Boltzmann, that concerned the
          delicate issue of the probability of collisions. It was
          this that transformed the time-symmetric theory of the
          collisions into the time-asymmetric theory that
          Boltzmann inadvertently developed. It is nevertheless
          remarkable how valuable Boltzmann’s theory, when
          applied appropriately, has proved to be.)
    - It was some time before the implications of this mismatch
      sank in. When they did, they very soon prompted Boltzmann
      to his greatest contribution to physics. That will be the
      topic of chapter 12; in the remainder of this chapter
      I will say a bit more about his 1872 paper and the
      remarkable consequences of a device he introduced in it to
      simplify and clarify his equations.

### Doppler Effect(!)
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/thermo/Julian-History-Thermo.txt
    - Buys Ballot made one of the most delightful and
      simultaneously hilarious experiments in physics. It was to
      test the effect predicted in 1842 by Christian Doppler.
    - In the modern age, we are all familiar with the way the
      pitch of police and ambulance sirens drops markedly as the
      emergency vehicles pass us. Of course, no such vehicles
      existed in the 1840s. Buys Ballot arranged for a band of
      brass musicians to stand on an open carriage of a steam
      train on the Utrecht–Amsterdam line. People on the station
      platform through which the train passed clearly heard that
      the pitch of the music was higher as the train approached
      and lower as it receded. If a reconstruction of the actual
      experiment is not on YouTube, it should be.

### Einstein and Copernicus
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/spacetime/einstein_companion.txt
    - Generally, a _Copernicus process_ can be conceived of as
      the _reorganization_ of a system of knowledge in which
      previously marginal elements take on a key role and serve
      as a starting point for a reinterpretation of the body of
      knowledge; typically _much of the technical apparatus is
      kept_, inference structures are reversed, and the previous
      conceptual foundation is discarded. Einstein's achievements
      during his miracle year of 1905 can be described in terms
      of such Copernicus processes.

### Light postulate: Source or Observer
    - The formulation of the light postulate, moreover, makes it
      clear that the experiment was not the origin of the
      postulate. The postulate does not say, as one would expect
      if it were linked to the Michelson-Morley experiment, that
      the velocity of light is independent of the motion of the
      _observer_ (although this is a direct consequence of the
      conjunction of the two postulates), but rather that it is
      independent of the motion of the _source_.

    - In fact, the result of the Michelson-Morley experiment
      would readily be explained if the velocity of light were
      dependent on the velocity of its _source_. The result of
      the experiment is thus perfectly compatible with the
      negation of the light postulate, which, all by itself,
      would seem to rule out that the postulate originated in the
      experiment.

    - The real origin of the light postulate, as Einstein made
      clear on many occasions, is to be found in _Maxwell's
      equations_ for electrodynamics. These equations predict the
      existence of electromagnetic waves propagating at the speed
      of light, regardless of the direction in which they travel
      and _regardless of the velocity of the source_ by which
      they are emitted.
        - (Like my vision of the two light beam from the
          Nullabour experiment)

### Kinetic energy, single particle
    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/Thermo/Energy-Subtle-Concept-Jennifer.pdf
    - Both proofs concern colliding bodies (or particles), but
      what about non-interacting bodies?
    - Consideration of this question leads to an enigma: it turns
      out to be impossible to prove (to derive from more
      elemental principles) the formula for the kinetic energy of
      a single iso- lated free particle. This is because the
      speed and direction of the isolated particle are relative
      to ‘absolute’ space whereas, for colliding particles, their
      velocities are, in effect, relative to each other.33 The
      curious end result is that the kinetic energy of an
      isolated free particle must simply be defined to be ½mv2.
      On the other hand, a system consisting entirely of nothing
      but one free particle is freakishly simple; and its very
      freakishness means it doesn’t really matter how its energy
      has been defined.

### Energy in the "field"
    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/Thermo/Energy-Subtle-Concept-Jennifer.pdf
    - There were other field theories apart from
      electromagnetism. In the early nineteenth century there was
      Green’s potential function, V(r) (see Chapter 13),
      a proto–field theory approach for ‘force fields’ such as
      gravity, electrostatics, and magnetostatics. It was used
      mostly by mathematicians (Green, Poisson, Gauss, and
      others), whereas ‘work [F.dr] was for the workers’5
      (engineers such as Coriolis, Navier, and Poncelet). Hooke’s
      Law of elasticity applied to a spring extended by
      a weight—but what about forces and materials that were
      extensive and continuous? Engineers realized that energy
      could be stored in a ‘con- tinuum’ (throughout the extended
      volume of a material), and so the energy density had to
      specified point by point, like a field property, and
      depended on the type of material, and whether it was
      stretched, squashed, or twisted, and whether electric or
      other external forces were applied. Augustin-Louis Cauchy
      (1789–1857) made a crucial advance in constructing
      a mathematical object,6 the stress energy tensor, that
      could store the information* in just the right way, with
      mathematical transformation properties built in (a bit like
      a modern-day computer spreadsheet). Finally, in Einstein’s
      Theory of General Relativity (1915) (see below), it was
      proposed that gravity exists as a field. (This also needed
      a stress energy tensor, showing a family connection between
      the theory of continuum mechanics and Einstein’s Theory of
      Gravitation.) Even more profound was Einstein’s realization
      that matter could emerge from the field, and that there was
      essentially no qualitative difference between matter and
      field: ‘Matter is where the concentration of energy is
      great, field where the concentration of energy is small’.7
      Today, there are modern field theories (for example,
      quantum field theory) and, as always, there is energy in
      the field.

### Bob Hanlon, Block-by-block, scientific interfaces
    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/Thermo/block-by-block-thermo-foundations.pdf
    - Why did I include the story of the Big Bang in this book?
    - First, because it explains the origins and populations of
      the elements in the periodic table.
    - Second, because the world of astronomy rarely overlaps with
      the world of physics/chemistry/engineering, and perhaps
      something, _some opportunity to gain insight_, is lost
      because of this.
    - Our division of science and engineering into different
      camps serves a purpose; we can’t study everything, but
      perhaps this does us an injustice as it narrows our view,
      and hence our understanding, and hence our ability to
      explore and create.
    - Look at Gamow’s story. He discovered something new based on
      combining particle physics with cosmology; it’s unfortunate
      his groundbreaking work didn’t receive proper recognition
      simply because he wasn’t part of the cosmology community.
    - Life at the _interface between two sciences_ can be fertile
      ground and also rather exciting. We’ll see this theme again
      in some of the stories to follow, such as those involving
      Galileo.

### GR doesn't conserve energy!
    - (key ideas:
        - no external agent or boundary in the universe, so not
          like a piston in a container...
        - conservation comes from _time reversal_ invariance of
          laws for the other 3 forces but not gravity / the
          universe)
    - As the universe expanded, space itself stretched and
      stretched the wavelength of the photons in turn, causing
      their energy to decrease and the universe to cool.
    - The cooling of the universe is sometimes confusingly
      attributed to the universe’s expansion from the standpoint
      that as a gas expands, it cools.
        - But this cause–effect logic doesn’t work.
        - While adiabatic expansion in a piston does lead to
          cooling, this is caused by the fact that the piston is
          doing work, thereby meaning that the gas molecules
          rebound from the retracting piston at a slower rate
          than when they struck the piston.
        - But there is no such piston at work in the universe.
          There is no boundary that the universe is expanding
          against.
    - So why then did the universe cool?
        - The increasing wavelength of photons with increasing
          expansion of the universe follows from applying
          Einstein’s GR.
    - And this naturally begs another question: how does the
      resulting energy balance work?
    - _Where does the energy lost by the photons end up going_?
    - It appears that Einstein’s equations are not consistent
      with the conservation of energy.
    - The _actual mechanism_ by which the stretching of space
      causes the stretching of photon wavelength is unknown and
      not currently consistent with the conservation of energy.
    - For a good discussion of this topic see Hawley and Holcomb
      pp. 414–415. Where does the energy lost by the photons go?
        - $MY_NOTES/physics/Energy-Gone.txt
        - $MY_BOOKS/Astro/cosmology-Foundations-Hawley-Holcomb.pdf
        - It seems to just disappear, but that is incompatible
          with energy conservation. However, we do not currently
          understand whether the law of conservation of energy
          applies to the universe as a whole or what a consistent
          definition of the total energy of the universe might
          be. Perhaps further research into the nature of space
          and time will explain this mystery)

### TESLA
    - His tremendous and troubled life
    - The enigmatic Nikola Tesla—stalked by his ever-present
      inner demons—invents the modern world. His astonishing
      story is that of a new-age god, a genius, a Zeus,
      a wonderful Wizard, yet a deeply troubled one.
    - He tames the mysterious force called “electricity;” he
      dazzles the world with his endless inventions and
      discoveries; he blazes new paths in science that profoundly
      impact our daily lives; he turns fantasies into realities;
      his thought experiments disrupt scientific norms; he gives
      us many of the indispensable tools we use today; and famous
      actresses and chanteuses clamor for his attention as
      powerful men desire to be his friend ... all before an
      astonished world.
    - Yet all the while he keeps his own counsel, as he
      simultaneously struggles with the challenging consequences
      of bipolar disorder: flights of manic energy alternating
      with depressive depths of great despair.
    - He shuns the clichés of a quotidian life, while forever
      seeking to:
        > lift the burdens from the shoulders of mankind
    - It would become his lifelong leitmotif, but at what cost to
      him?

### Understood probability 'amplitude'
    - July 2022: finally understood QM `amplitude` today.
    - Never really took notice of the word amplitude fully.
    - It's just the distance of a wave from the central line...
        - Taken from: Tim Maudlin / John Horgan discussion
        - Don't call the 'wave function' a probability _WAVE_.
        - Call it a probability _amplitude_ because you have to
          square it to get a probability.
        - You never get destructive interference between
          probabilities, because probabilities are always
          positive.


### Epicycles
    - HN: QM dead end
      https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=30607692
    - We've been in an "epicyclean phase" of physics for some
      time, trapped by the huge predictive success of QED and
      still using mathematical methods devised in the 19th
      century (variational calculus). This has lead us to the
      current situation, with extremely complicated theories at
      the limit of human understanding that bear no new results.
    - It will take a modern day Copernicus to come up with a new
      view of physics, that will result in simpler, more
      productive models, to take us out of the local maximum we
      are in.
    - Reply
        - Copernicus' model had the planets orbiting the same in
          circles at uniform speed. This doesn't match reality,
          so Copernicus just chucked in some epicycles to correct
          the error. Copernicus's model had all the complexity of
          epicycles, but he hard-coded the first epicycle into
          the system by having the planets orbit the Sun instead
          of the Earth.
        - We wouldn't need a Copernicus to solve this problem,
          we'd need a Copernicus, then a Kepler, (ellipses
          & non-uniform speed) then a Newton, (gravity causes
          ellipses and non-uniform speed) and then an Einstein.
          (gravity is warping of space-time)
        - If QFT is as wrong as Ptolemy's geocentric model was,
          we're hopeless. Because QFT predicts the observations
          very well; our observations have no ellipses in them
          that invalidate circular orbits, our observations have
          no anomalous Mercury precession that invalidates
          Newtonian gravity, no speed of light being consistent
          in all directions to invalidate luminiferous aether. To
          say that we simply need a smarter theoretical physicist
          is simply wrong; our current theories do not contradict
          the things we're able to observe.
        - We know that GR and QM don't play nice at small scales
          and high local gravity. But we can't observe this
          conflict. There's nothing to go on.
        - We'd need an observation which shows that GR or QFT is
          wrong about something before we could conceivably make
          foundational progress on new or different theories
          (NO). And every few months there's a new article about
          "Einstein is proven right again" or "LHC experiment
          shows all readings are nominal".
        - Reply 1
            - So what is dark matter?
            - This thing keeps galaxies bound; we cannot see it
              but only observe its effects… I think the hunt for
              it will push us into new territory.
            - That being said, there has been nothing yet found
              that violates GR/QFT as you say.
        - Reply 2
            > we don't really have any experimental data that
            > can't be explained by the Standard Model
            - What was the evidence Copernicus saw that epicycles
              didn't explain? (Honest question.)
    - Another Comment:
    - Could modern-day Copernicii be stymied by modern-day
      academic funding structures and grant review committees?
        - Reply: I also have this impression. I think the current

          if they get away from the herd, they have a hard time
          joining a big research group, getting funds and getting
          more people to read and cite their papers.

### Great research
    - HN: On the use of a life (2020)
      https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=31981486
    - There's a big truth here that screams out at me. If you
      want to do great work, you need _freedom_ first.
    - reply
        - If you pay a man a salary for doing research, both of
          you will want something to show at the end of the year,
          to prove money has not been wasted.
        - In promising work of the highest class, however,
          results do not come in this regular fashion, in fact
          _years may pass_ without any tangible result being
          obtained, and the position of the paid worker would be
          very embarrassing and he would naturally take to work
          on a lower, or at any rate different plane where he'd
          be sure of getting tangible results year-by-year which
          would justify his salary.
        - The position is this: You want one kind of research,
          but, if you pay someone to do it, it will drive them to
          research of a different kind. The only thing to do is
          to _pay them for doing something else_ and give them
          enough leisure to do research for the love of it.
            - from the biography of J. J. Thompson

### Origin of all fundamental forces
    - Jakob CM $MY_NOTES/physics/CM/Jakob-CM.txt
    - All fundamental forces originate from an _underlying_
      potential
    - More precisely:
        - forces that originate from an underlying potential are
          called _conservative forces_, because the work done by
          them as we move an object around a closed curve is
          zero. ie no (kinetic?) energy gets lost or added.
    - Conservative forces only dependent on location `q` and not
      velocity `q̇`

### Bayesian Stats and String Theory
    - https://www.math.columbia.edu/~woit/wordpress/?p=12910#comments
    - Peter,
    - “I went back and took a look again at Polchinski’s Bayesian
      calculations. His Bayesian method is that each argument he
      has in favor of something reduces the probability that it
      is wrong by 1/2.”
    - This line of thinking exhibits almost too many fallacies to
      count: it implicitly assumes a discrete state space, then
      employs equiprobability with only two possible outcomes
      (along the lines of “you’re either gonna roll sixes or not,
      it’s 50/50”), obscures the distinction between scientific
      and statistical significance, mixes up the p-value with the
      probability that the null hypothesis is true, says nothing
      regarding the power of the test, uses likelihood to mean
      posterior, the list goes on and on.
    - All that being said, the reason I am writing this comment
      is to emphasize that Polchinski was facing major challenges
      when he produced this alleged argument. While it’s true
      that in science we’re supposed to focus on the argument not
      the person, to me at least it feels infelicitous (to put it
      mildly) to dissect this specific argument.

### Machs Principle (Jakob)
    - (interesting: I always thought of Machs principle in
      relation to Newtons bucket, not really as the equivalence
      of inertial and gravitational mass)
    - You may wonder how the two descriptions of mass (as
      "gravitational charge" and as "resistance to being pushed
      around") actually fit together.
    - Historically physicists used the terms "gravitational
      mass" and "intertial mass" because they thought the
      2 different roles of mass meant there was actually 2 kinds
      of mass.
    - However the idea that gravitational mass and interial mass
      are the same thing (now simply called mass) was a key
      insight leading Einstein to GR.
    - From this perspective, the resistance of an object to being
      moved around (ie inertial mass) is a result of the
      gravitational pull of all the objects in the whole
      universe. (Machs Principle)


### Symmetries: Rickles basic philosophy
    - Key: modern meaning: `symmetry = TRANSFORMATION` that
      leaves some property invariant.
    - Originally 'symmetry' meant harmonious, balanced or
      well-proportioned. (Greek word: 'same measure')
        - But this is in a static sense: a figure, building etc
          can be symmetric without us doing anything.
    - But in modern terms, a 'symmetry' means observing something
      before and after some ACTION has been performed on it.
    - It's kind of a 'change without a change': operations or
      _transformations_ that leave something, or everything,
      about an object the same as before.
    - It indicates a kind of 'insensitivity' of some properties
      or relations to some transformations that might rotate,
      shuffle, move, twist, push or modify a system in some way.
    - In such cases, the TRANSFORMATIONS for which this holds
      true are called 'symmetries' and the object is said to be
      invariant (or symmetric) under the transformation.

### Asimov
    - Wolf: Sometimes it's hard to be certain exactly what a
      law of nature is.
    - Asimov: That's why it's better to have some background in
      science, not necessarily a formal one; you can work up
      a background _on your own_.
    - People forget that the great revolutionaries of science
      thoroughly knew what they were revolutionizing.
    - In other words, Galileo knew Aristotelian physics. Vesalius
      knew Galenic anatomy, and Newton understood all the
      theories of the universe.
    - No one has revolutionized science by not knowing what came
      before. Many people who write me letters with brand-new
      theories of the universe haven't the foggiest idea what the
      present theories are. And you just can't do it that way.

### Physical foundations of GR (Sciama)
    - More and more I'm thinking of a 'plenum' through the
      universe... or absolute space is substantial.
    - But read this at mass this eve: (Sciama)
        - Describes Newton's bucket experiment. Then...
        - Newton's concept of absolute space has several
          unsatisfactory features. Firstly, we cannot think of
          absolute space as consisting of some sort of "stuff"
          permeating the universe(!)
    - For then we would expect _absolute velocity_ to have
      a meaning as well as absolute acceleration, and we would
      have no principle of relativity at all, no equivalence of
      inertial observers.
    - How should we understand that uniform motion through
      absolute space has no observable effects, but acceleration
      does have?
    - The reason Newton couldn't answer this is because the
      introduction of absolute space doesn't really provide
      a physical explanation for the preferred role of inertial
      frames. It's simply a restatement of their existence.
    - For the same reason, it leads to the paradox that absolute
      space can act on matter through inertial forces, without
      itself being acted on by matter... since in Newton's
      conception, the properties of absolute space are fixed
      a-priori, independently of the matter it contains.
    - To construct a _physical theory of inertia_, we can proceed
      in 2 ways:
        - We regard "space" as the source of inertial forces and
          ascribe physical prpoerties to it in the sense that it
          not be absolute and fixed, but instead has _variable_
          properties because of the back-action on it from the
          matter on which it acts.
            - This abstract viewpoint corresponds to the field
              concept of physical interactions. (e.g. charges
              don't interact directly, but through an electric
              field. The emphasis is on the local aspects of the
              interaction... the electric force acting on
              a charge only depends on the field in its immediate
              neighborhood)
            - Actually this was the point of view adopted by
              Einstein
        - Another approach is "direct connection". e.g. Coulombs
          Law approach in constrast to Maxwell's fields.
            - In the intertial case, we'd say the inertial forces
              are exerted by _other bodies_, not by space.
            - If this makes sense, then inertial forces aren't
              fictitious at all, but are just as physical as
              other forces.
            - In consequence, Newton's laws would hold in all
              frames of reference, and the problem of the
              preferred role of inertial frames would be solved.
            - These frames would just be those where the inertial
              forces are zero, ie, just those disposed in some
              particular way relative to the bodies exerting
              these forces.
            - But what are these bodies? We consider this in the
              next chapter.

### Not even wrong: Oct 2021
    - Gian Giudice has put on the arXiv a written version of his
      Theory closing talk at LCHP2021. He ends with
        - These are interesting times for particle physics: times
          of great uncertainty, in which our physics perspective
          is changing, and in which we are laying the foundations
          for the future of our field. As a community, we must
          rise to the challenge.
    - What worries me is that the much of the rest of the article
      contains a lot of multiverse pseudo-science:
        - The multiverse describes a physical reality that
          challenges the presumption that there must be a single
          unified theory in the deep UV. In a sense, it is the
          ultimate Copernican revolution since not even the patch
          of the universe we live in is special. It implies
          a revision of the cosmological principle because the
          universe is approximately homogeneous and isotropic
          only within our horizon, but may be globally highly
          non-homogeneous. The multiverse is not an abstract
          idea, but it is a generic consequence of a large class
          of inflationary theories, where unavoidable quantum
          fluctuations of the inflaton spark a chain process with
          eternal creation of regions that expand faster than the
          surrounding space. The multiverse is actually
          a familiar instrument of our everyday physics toolkit.

    - Arguing against the fundamental significance of symmetry
      principles:
        - There are also theoretical indications for questioning
          the concept of symmetry. It is nowbelieved (and to
          a certain extent proven) that any global symmetry is
          violated at the level of quantum gravity. This means
          that any global symmetry that we observe in nature is
          only an accidental effect of looking at a system
          without sufficient short-distance resolution. The case
          of gauge symmetries is more subtle. Gauge symmetries
          are not real physical symmetries, in the sense that
          they don’t correspond to an invariance under a physical
          transformation, but only to a redundancy of the
          coordinate parametrisation.
        - We often confuse our students on this point by showing
          them the Mexican-hat potential and leading them to
          believe that there is a degeneracy of vacua, when in
          reality there is only one single vacuum state that
          breaks EW symmetry, as it is clear from the fact that
          the physical spectrum doesn’t contain any Goldstone
          boson corresponding to zero-energy excitations. Gauge
          symmetries may not be as fundamental as we thought, but
          only an emergent phenomenon. They could be a mirage of
          a different reality that takes place at a more
          fundamental level.

### How Einstein Created Relativity
    - The idea that motion is a “state” like rest was his unique
      conceptualization

    - Thus we arrive at the non-causal concept of inertia, and
      there is no need to posit some infinite internal power
      Today’s definition of inertia in any textbook is a direct
      descendent of Newton’s. Inertia is still _a non-causal
      concept of motion_. From this point of view there is no
      answer to the question, “What is inertia?” – beyond its
      role as a description of how bodies behave when moving or
      at rest.8

              obviously no deficiencies,
        - and the other way is to make it so complicated that
          there are no obvious deficiencies.

      inspiration as the discovery of the _simple physical laws_
      which underlie the complex phenomena of nature.

### Barbour Time
    - In many senses, declaring time to be illusory was the
      low-hanging fruit. Our deepest theories suggest that time
      is not fundamental to the universe: It doesn’t really
      figure, except as a marker of change, in quantum physics.
    - In relativity it is malleable, its moments undefinable
      across the universe.
    - The remaining big question is what causes our experience of
      time’s arrow? The one-way flow of moments?
    - Barbour has explained what he thinks the answer is in 'The
      Janus Point'.
    - That answer, he said, has lain in plain sight, waiting to
      be discovered since the 18th century.

### Kepler Light
    - One of the first people to study light scientifically at
      this time was Johannes Kepler. In 1604 he published
      a treatise on optics whree he described a fundamental law
      of light.
    - He said that the intensity of light weakened with the
      square of the distance from which it was observed. So if
      you move a candle ten feet away, you perceive its light to
      be one-hundredth as bright as it was when you were standing
      right next to it.
    - Then, in the 1670s, while conducting experiments with
      prisms, Isaac Newton discovered that light is composed of
      colors and that red and blue light make purple light.

### Kadanoff Phase changes
    - Kadanoff's early research focused upon superconductivity.
    - In the late 1960s, he studied the organization of matter in
      phase transitions. He showed that sudden changes in
      material properties (such as the magnetization of a magnet
      or the boiling of a fluid) could be understood in terms of
      _scaling_ and _universality_.
    - With his collaborators, he showed how experimental data
      available for the changes, called second-order phase
      transitions, could be understood in terms of these two
      ideas.
    - $MY_IMAGES/physics/phase-transitions.jpg
    - See also: ./thermo/Wilson-Phase-Transitions.txt
        - His analysis showed that sufficiently close to the
          critical point, most system variables become redundant.
          The critical phenomena are essentially determined by
          _two numbers_:
        - the `dimensionality` of the system (1, 2 or 3)
        - the dimensionality of a key quantity: `order parameter`
          (introduced in Landau’s theory)

### Work more important than force
    - The innumerable cases where the PVW succeeds and Newtonian
      Mechanics fails shows us that it is indeed _work_ rather
      than force that is pre-eminent.
    - In simple problems, in rectangular coordinates, it would be
      a mistake not to use Newtonian methods and benefit from the
      physically intuitive forces, but we must always remember
      “force is a secondary quantity derived from... work.”
    - As Lanczos said:
        > “we are inclined to believe that force is something
        > primitive andirreducible, [however] . . . it is not the
        > force but the work done by the force which is of
        > primary importance.”
    - This is the main reason for using the PVW: it is abstract,
      intuitively opaque, and complicated mathematically, but its
      the way the physical world really _is_.


### Lazy Universe take note:
    - Lanczos Posulate A: reaction forces
    - also these interesting questions:
    - There are some questions we’re not allowed to ask:
    - Why does the P of VW work?
        - Answer: we don’t know, it just does.
    - How do the forces-of-reaction act cooperatively?
        - Answer: we don’t know, they just do
    - How can a test in a fictitious space tell us about the real
      world?
        - We don’t know, but it happens also in countless other
          circumstances.
    - But this is only half the answer.
    - The utility of using a virtual space is that it can provide
      an absolute benchmark in a way no actual physical space
      could ever do.
    - This absoluteness and invariance of results in the abstract
      test space is what comes about in ‘differential geometry’
      (which used to be called ‘absolute geometry’).
    - The results are the same irrespective of coordinates or how
      the system was modelled.
    - This remarkable invariance is a feature of the whole of
      Variational Mechanics (the P of VW, Principle of Least
      Action, Lagrangian Mechanics, and Hamiltonian Mechanics)
    - Now the fact that the PVW yields absolute answers, that’s
      mathematics, but the fact that it’s _work_ that’s minimized
      as opposed to some other quantity - that’s physics.

### XYLYXYLYX Why does it Move?
    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/CM/XYLYXYLYX-Why-Does-It-Move-Aug-2020.pdf
    - This monograph was a long hard answer to a simple question.
    - Any inefficiencies in the exposition are intended to
      provide a solid review, a fresh perspective, or a deeper
      mathematical exploration for those familiar with GR.
    - The presentation of the Schwarzschild geodesics using the
      modern formalism of Carlson integrals was an extension of
      Kostic 2012, which we analysed and explained in detail.
    - The ultimate answer to the question, “Why does it move?” is
      presented entirely in a single picture, `Figure 5` which
      could have been presented and explained in a single
      paragraph.
        - $MY_IMAGES/physics/xylyxylyx-key-figure-5.jpg
    - Our obsessive requirement that each element of that diagram
      be motivated and mathematically rigourous led to the length
      of this document.
    - But, in a sentence:
        > “It falls to the center because time ﬂows.”
    - As proper time ticks on, the worldline weaves into the
      future and is directed through space according to the
      geodesic equations.
    - Our embedding diagram is merely a projection: a projection
      of the something real, the worldline, onto something
      ﬁctitious, a metaphor for space called the “embedding
      diagram.”

### Lagrangian comment
    - from a classical mechanics point of view, the principle of
      least action (and the minus sign) is understandable.
    - If KE is becoming PE, that means the object is getting
      slower.
    - If PE is becoming KE, the object is getting faster.
    - So, minimizing `KE - PE` means PE and KE try not to switch
      between each other...
    - objects speed up and slow down as little as possible...
    - ie., objects in motion stay in motion...
    - objects at rest stay at rest.
    - First law all over again.

### cosmic birefringence
    - ./cosmo/Sean-Carroll-birefringence.txt
    - Could hint at a breaking in Lorentz invariance and also
      a preferred direction in space
    - Every photon has a direction of polarization, which
      generally remains fixed as the photon travels through
      space.
    - Birefringence is an effect by which the polarization
      rotates rather than staying fixed. It can happen in
      materials, but generally not in outer space. But there are
      exotic physics ideas that could cause such a rotation,
      including the dynamical dark energy candidate known as
      quintessence

### Dirac
    - His last paper (1984), entitled "The inadequacies of
      quantum field theory," contains his final judgment on
      quantum field theory: "These rules of renormalisation give
      surprisingly, excessively good agreement with experiments.
      Most physicists say that these working rules are,
      therefore, correct. I feel that is not an adequate reason.
      Just because the results happen to be in agreement with
      observation does not prove that one's theory is correct."
      The paper ends with the words: "I have spent many years
      searching for a Hamiltonian to bring into the theory and
      have not yet found it. I shall continue to work on it as
      long as I can and other people, I hope, will follow along
      such lines."

### Motl Vacuum
    - Dear Eliáš, now you talk about the ambiguity of the vacuum
      state, not the whole reps.
    - The vacuum state MEANS the ground state of the energy, the
      eigenstate with the lowest eigenvalue. If there is no
      unambiguous Hamiltonian, there can be no unambiguous
      vacuum. Common sense.
    - There is no privileged definition of a Hamiltonian in
      a generic curved spacetime. So there is clearly no unique
      vacuum state there.
    - These statements are facts deduced from the long distance
      approximation within its range of validity (they really
      follow from the general principles of relativity) and they
      cannot be changed by a more precise theory. In a fancier
      theory, there can be a detailed discussion of vacuum like
      states but not a unique one.
    - In special relativity, energy is P0, a component of
      a 4-vector, so there is a dependence on the inertial frame,
      mixing with the momentum. General relativity allows
      "different speeds at different points of space" and mixing
      the Hamiltonians for different inertial observers at
      various points. That's why in a generic background without
      special-relativity-like asymptotia, there is a natural
      non-uniqueness of the Hamiltonian. These claims don't
      really depend on the precise dynamical theory or UV
      completions. They are statements that follow from the
      general principles of relativity that we want any good
      theory to reproduce in one way or another.
    - De Sitter space has a special symmetry and that is why one
      can talk about various vacuum-like states there but not
      a truly "unique vacuum".

    - Another comment:

          quite good, I strongly recommend it.”
        - In contrast to you, I strongly unrecommend it. It is
          very misleading in giving virtual particles an

          https://www.physicsforums.com/insights/vacuum-fluctuation-myth/
          and

        - https://www.cambridge.org/nu/academic/subjects/physics/theoretical-physics-and-mathematical-physics/diagrammatica-path-feynman-diagrams
        - is a textbook on quantum field theory covering the
          standard ground up to QED in an essentially standard
          manner. This technically precise (though not
          mathematically rigorous) book contains not a single
          mention of the word ‘virtual’. Instead, Veldman
          explains here in an exemplary way what Feynman diagrams
          are and how they are interpreted and used. From the
          introduction:
        - ”Perturbation theory means Feynman diagrams. It appears
          therefore that anyone working in elementary particle
          physics, experimentalist or theorist, needs to know
          about these objects. […] This then is the aim: to make
          it clear which principles are behind the rules, and to
          define clearly the calculational details. This requires
          some kind of derivation. The method used is basically
          the canonical formalism, but anything that is not
          strictly necessary has been cut out. No one should have
          an excuse not understanding this book. Knowing about
          ordinary non-relativistic QM and
          classical relativity one should be able to understand
          the reasoning.”


### Anderson
    - Anderson's relationship with particle physicists was
      contentious. In the 1960's, he complains, they "were
      claiming on all sides that they were doing the fundamental
      science, and what the rest of us were doing was just
      engineering."

    - Anderson challenged this assertion in his 1972 "More Is
      Different" article, pointing out that reality has
      a hierarchical structure, with each level independent, to
      some degree, of the levels above and below. "At each stage,
      entirely new laws, concepts and generalizations are
      necessary, requiring inspiration and creativity to just as
      great a degree as in the previous one," Anderson argued.
      "Psychology is not applied biology, nor is biology applied
      chemistry."

### Kepler: Analogies
    - And I cherish more than anything else the Analogies, my
      most trustworthy masters.
    - They know all the secrets of Nature, and they ought to be
      least neglected in Geometry.

### Analog models of gravity
    - ... are attempts to model various phenomena of GR (e.g.,
      black holes or cosmological geometries) using other
      physical systems such as acoustics in a moving fluid,
      superfluid helium, or Bose–Einstein condensate; gravity
      waves in water; and propagation of EM waves in a dielectric
      medium.
    - These analogs (or analogies) provide new ways of looking at
      problems, permit ideas from other realms of science to be
      applied, and may create experiments within the analogue
      that can be applied back to the source phenomena.
    - Living Review Analog Gravity
      https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5255896/

### Superfluid Vacuum theory
    - Superfluid vacuum theory (SVT), sometimes known as the BEC
      vacuum theory, is an approach in theoretical physics and
      QM where the fundamental physical vacuum
      (non-removable background) is viewed as superfluid or as
      a Bose–Einstein condensate (BEC).
    - The concept of a luminiferous aether as a medium sustaining
      electromagnetic waves was discarded after the advent of SR,
      as the presence of the concept alongside SR leads to
      several contradictions;
    - in particular, aether having a definite velocity at each
      space-time point will exhibit a _preferred direction_.
    - This conflicts with the relativistic requirement that all
      directions within a light cone are equivalent.
    - However, as early as in 1951 Dirac published two papers
      where he pointed out that we should take into account
      quantum fluctuations in the flow of the aether.[1][2] His
      arguments involve the application of the uncertainty
      principle to the velocity of aether at any space-time
      point, implying that the velocity will not be
      a well-defined quantity. In fact, it will be distributed
      over various possible values. At best, one could represent
      the aether by a wave function representing the perfect
      vacuum state for which all aether velocities are equally
      probable.

### Cavendish: Great scientist... extraordinary peculiarities
    - Newton, whose writings Cavendish studied closely, wrote in
      his optical treatise that “Nature will be very conformable
      to herself and very simple.”
    - Newton associated simplicity with truth: “Truth is ever to
      be found in the simplicity, and not in the multiplicity and
      confusion of things.”
    - Cavendish, in a plan of a treatise on mechanics, wrote that
      Newton’s 2nd law of motion is “the most simple & therefore
      the most likely to be true of any law one can invent.”
    - Truth is simple, nature is simple; simplicity was
      Cavendish’s preference in all things.

### Bala Poisson Brackets
    - on it's own the Poisson bracket can look unnatural...
    - if you have a symplectic space / metric and 2 constants of
      motion, `f` and `g` the Poisson bracket {f,g} is the
      symplectic dot product of the gradients of these two
      functions in phase space
    - and if you know the gradiants of two functions are
      perpendicular to each other, it means the level sets of `f`
      and `g` are perpendicular. The same thing is true here
      except with the Symplectic metric
    -  you can say what two things are in "involution"
     - this algebraic structure that the Poisson bracket carries
       with it, they're very useful, they provide transparent,
       geometrical physical pictures of what is going on
     - Bala mentioned Betrands theorem too: all bounded orbits
       are closed orbits

### Equation of state (EOS)
    - an equation showing the relationship between the pressure,
      volume, and temperature of a quantity of a substance.
    - In thermodynamics, an EOS is a thermodynamic equation
      relating state variables which describe the state of matter
      under a given set of physical conditions, such as pressure,
      volume, temperature (PVT), or internal energy.
    - Equations of state are useful in describing the properties
      of fluids, mixtures of fluids, solids, and the interior of
      stars.

### Local v Global Physics
    - https://physics.stackexchange.com/questions/77368/intuitively-why-are-bundles-so-important-in-physics/77412#77412
    - https://ncatlab.org/nlab/show/fiber+bundles+in+physics
    - All of physics has two aspects:
        - a local, even infinitesimal aspect,
        - and a global aspect.
    - Much of traditional lore deals just with local and
      infinitesimal aspects (perturbative aspects)
    - Fiber bundles play little role there.
    - But fiber bundles are the all-important structure that
      govern the global (non-perturbative) aspects.
    - Bundles are the global structure of physical fields and are
      irrelevant only for crude local (perturbative) descriptions
      of reality.

### The gauge/gravity duality
    - Living review: Numerical Relativity
    - https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/lrr-2015-1
    - The gauge/gravity duality, or AdS/CFT correspondence, is
      the conjecture proposed by Maldacena in 1998 [536], and
      further developed in [798, 372], that string theory on an
      AdS spacetime (times a compact space) is dual (i.e.,
      equivalent under an appropriate mapping) to a CFT defined
      on the boundary of the AdS space.
    - Since its proposal, this conjecture has been supported by
      impressive and compelling evidence, it has branched off to,
      e.g., the AdS/Condensed Matter correspondence [396], and it
      has inspired other proposals of duality relations with
      a similar spirit, such as the dS/CFT correspondence [731]
      and the Kerr/CFT correspondence [373].
    - All these dualities are examples of the holographic
      principle, which has been proposed in the context of
      quantum gravity [737, 734], stating that the information
      contained in a D-dimensional gravitational system is
      encoded in some boundary of the system. The paradigmatic
      example of this idea is a BH spacetime, whose entropy is
      proportional to the horizon area.

### Interesting: two-body is important!
    - A recurring theme of this course has been approximation
      methods, so you may be wondering why we are so concerned
      with one of the few exactly solvable cases in physics.
    - Remember anytime we perform a perturbative calculation, we
      need an exactly solvable case to perturb away from.
    - Many problems in astronomy (and physics in general) can be
      well-modelled by _starting_ with the solution to some sort
      of _two-body problem_... then perturbing away from it.
    - This makes the two-body problem all the more important to
      understand.

### When is a force CONSERVATIVE?
    - 1D:
        - a force `F(x)` is conservative if `F(x)` is only
          a function of POSITION alone.
    - 2D / 3D:
        - it is necessary, but not sufficient, that `F(r)` is
          only a function of the position.
        - There are forces `F(r)` that are only functions of
          position, but NOT actually conservative.
            - Example: magnetic force
                - $MY_NOTES/physics/EM/Magnetic-force-not-conservative.txt
        - So for a force to be truly conservative, it must be the
          GRADIENT of a POTENTIAL: `F = -∇U`

### What is sound
    - Sound is acoustic waves in a medium. Acoustic waves are
      adiobatic compression/rarefaction waves.
    - The medium, and whatever objects it exists as, are not
      sound itself. The notional particles of sound waves are
      called _phonons_.
    - Sound is an example of density waves.
    - The waves at the interface between the ocean and the air
      are gravity waves. Both are physical.

      heading.

### Geometrized unit system
    - A geometrized unit system or geometric unit system is
      a system of _natural units_ where the base physical units
      are chosen to ensure the speed of light in vacuum, `c`, and
      the gravitational constant, `G`, are equal to one.

    - The geometrized unit system is not a completely defined system.
      Some other systems are geometrized unit systems in the sense
      that they set these and also other constants to unity. e.g.
      Stoney units and Planck units.

    - This system is useful especially in SR and GR. All physical
      quantities are identified with _geometric quantities_ such as
      areas, lengths, dimensionless numbers, path curvatures, or
      sectional curvatures.  (Ah everything is like a length?)
