### A comment from years ago I always wondered about
    - "...Einstein stopped in this tracks"
    - Gravity Is Not Like the Other Forces (quantamagazine.org)
    - https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=23537334
    - ./spacetime/hn-gravity-not-like-other-forces.txt
        > its estimated if you add up the energy in star,
        > including the matter it's composed of, and deduct the
        > negative energy of it's gravitational field they cancel
        > out
    - No, not true.
    - If it were, the star's mass would be zero.
    - The meaning of "gravitational potential energy is negative"
      is that the mass of the star is less than the total (sum)
      mass of all of its constituents.
    - Alternatively: if we make a star out of a highly diffuse
      cloud of atoms as you describe, the process will have to
      release energy (by emitting radiation into space that
      escapes the star system). But the energy left over is far
      from zero: the mass of the star is energy.
        > It seems likely the net energy of the universe may well
        > be very close to zero.
    - This is a speculative hypothesis not the same as the usual
      concept of "gravitational potential energy" being negative.
      The fact that it's often framed in similar terminology is
      misleading.


    > No, this is not true. If it were, the star's mass would be
    > zero.
    - All it means is if we were to take all the constituent
      particles in a star and spread them out at 'infinity' from
      each other (effectively all across the universe), pushing
      them apart, working against the gravity of the star holding
      it together, the energy required would be equal to the
      energy in those particles (from their mass, nuclear forces,
      etc).
    - The energy cost of disassembling a star equals the energy
      in the star, so the net energy of the star is zero.
    - It's just a matter of doing the relevant calculations to
      see this, which was first done by Pascual Jordan in the
      1940s.
    - I realise it's a ridiculously counter-intuitive result.
      Apparently when Einstein was told this, _he stopped dead in
      his tracks_ while crossing a busy road.


### Tevian-Dray
    - $MY_NOTES/physics/metaphysics/tevian-dray-maths-v-physics.txt
    - We have argued [6] that mathematicians emphasize the
      `symbolic` manipulation of functions, whereas physicists
      care more about equations relating `PHYSICAL` quantities.
    - Temperature may be expressed by different functions in
      rectangular and polar coordinates, but these functions
      represent the same physical quantity, opening the door to
      a dispute [4,8,16,21] over whether both of these functions
      can be called `T`.
    - Mathematicians and physicists may share a common
      vocabulary, but don't use the same grammar.
    - Minor disputes over language reflect a deeper difference in
      viewpoint which we characterize by saying that Physicists
      do geometry, but Mathematicians teach algebra.
    - Geometry is the study of invariant objects, whose algebraic
      description might involve a choice of coordinates;
    - Both geometers and physicists have a notion of temperature
      `T` defined at a point that is more fundamental than any
      coordinate description.

### OU Astrophysics
    - "A radian is dimensionless (but not unitless)"
    - me: Thought they were the same thing??
        - later: a dimension seems to be a "direction" you can go
          in the physical world... and units the way you assign
          a number to that "dimension" direction.
            - Eg Length is a "dimension",
            - but you could have units of "feet" or "meters"
              etc...
    - Quora snippet:
        - Dimensions are made up of fundamental units of mass,
          length, time [MLT] etc.
        - Without units we wont have MLT.
        - But the opposite can be true . Angle = arc/radius, both
          arc and radius are lengths. so angle is dimensionless.
          But its unit is radian or degree.
    - Quora snippet:
        - A “unit” is a particular choice how to measure
          a “dimension”. E.g.
        - Seconds and years are different units,
            - but they both measure the same DIMENSION: time.
        - Miles and nanometers are different units,
            - but they both measure the same DIMENSION: length.
        - Acres and centimeters-squared are different units,
            - but they measure the same DIMENSION: area
              (=length^2)
    - Quora snippet:
        - Not all Dimensionless are unitless.
        - They may have unit or may not.
    - Examples of physical quantities that are dimesionless but
      have UNITS: (usually a ratio or product)
        - Physical quantity       Unit
        - -----------------       ----
        - Angular displacement    Radian
        - Refractive index        Kilometres per second
        - Atomic mass (unit?)     AMU = 1.66054 x 10-27kg


### Essays Frontiers Modern Astrophysics
    - The speed of light is a constant. In fact, this statement
      is incomplete. The speed of light is a constant as long as
      it remains in the same material medium.
    - Whenever light enters a denser or lighter medium, its speed
      and wavelength change, though its FREQUENCY STAYS THE SAME.
      (???)
        - if the wavelength changes, doesn't the frequency
          automatically change too?
        - https://physics.stackexchange.com/questions/59469/why-doesnt-the-frequency-of-light-change-during-refraction
        - Think of it like this: At the boundary/interface of the
          medium, the number of waves you send in is the number
          of waves you receive, at the other side, almost
          instantly. Frequency doesn't change because it depends
          on travelling of waves across the interface.
        - But speed and wavelength change as the material on the
          other side may be different, so now it might have
          a longer/shorter size of wave and so the NUMBER OF
          WAVES PER UNIT TIME CHANGES.
        - I thought number of waves per unit time IS the
          frequency? No.. must be wrong... frequency is just the
          time taken for one full "cycle"
        - later: ah the SPEED does change! When light enters
          a different medium... but the freq stays the same.
    - This explains why the speed of light is slow in water
      compared to air or a vacuum. The amount of slow down
      depends on the properties of the material medium, known as
      the refractive index.

    - Can we go further? No, going further would require giving
      up division. Actually the four algebras `R`, `C`, `H` and
      `O` are the only ones without zero divisors, that is,
      non-zero elements whose product is nonetheless zero.
    - The octonions are thus nature's largest division algebra.

      ultimately be seen as the key to understanding the basic
      building blocks of nature, namely fundamental particles
      such as electrons and quarks.

### Blasjo review
    - Isaac Newton and Natural Philosophy (Renaissance Lives)
    - Newton’s interest in esoteric and religious subjects seems
      genuine, whereas he ended up in mathematics almost
      involuntarily, “not due to any choice on his part but to
      the fact that his mind was extraordinarily equipped for
      mathematical inventiveness” (35).
    - Once in mathematics, Newton let it dictate the lead in all
      his work to an unprecedented extent. He dismissed
      qualitative and philosophical modes of doing science,
      deriding such speculations as “no difficult matter” (88).
    - Instead he held that “mathematical regularities deduced
      with certainty from phenomena” (88) were sufficient in
      themselves, even though this meant his science was “devoid
      of physical meaning” (8) in the eyes of many
      contemporaries.
    - Philosophy was a necessity for Newton rather than
      a vocation, a defensive strategy rather than a chosen line
      of research.”
    - When he did engage in it, his method was to extrapolate
      philosophical conclusions from mathematical results

### Infinite Series are important!
    - Perhaps the most widely used technique in the physicist’s
      toolbox is the use of inﬁnite series (i.e., sums consisting
      formally of an inﬁnite number of terms) to represent
      functions, to bring them to forms facilitating further
      analysis, or even as a prelude to numerical eval-uation.
      The acquisition of skill in creating and manipulating
      series expansions is therefore an absolutely essential part
      of the training of one who seeks competence in the
      mathemat-ical methods of physics, and it is therefore the
      ﬁrst topic in this text. An important part of this skill
      set is the ability to recognize the functions represented
      by commonly encountered expansions, and it is also of
      importance to understand issues related to the convergence
      of inﬁnite series.

### SICM
    - Classical mechanics is deceptively simple. It is
      surprisingly easy to get the right answer with fallacious
      reasoning or without real understanding.
    - Traditional mathematical notation contributes to this
      problem. Symbols have ambiguous meanings that depend on
      context, and often change even within a given context.
    - Eg, a fundamental result of mechanics is the Lagrange
      equations. In traditional notation the Lagrange equations
      are written ...
    - The Lagrangian `L` must be interpreted as a function of the
      position and velocity components `q^i` and `qdot^i`, so
      that the partial derivatives make sense, but then in order
      for the time derivative `d/dt` to make sense, solution
      paths must have been inserted into the partial derivatives
      of the Lagrangian to make functions of time.
    - The traditional use of ambiguous notation is convenient in
      simple situations, but in more complicated situations it
      can be a serious handicap to clear reasoning.
    - In order that the reasoning be clear and unambiguous, we
      have adopted a more precise mathematical notation. Our
      notation is functional and follows that of modern
      mathematical presentations.

### Noether
    - (from Harvey Brown - Physical Relativity)
    - In 1918, the same year as the Kretschmann debate, Noether
      published a peper on the role of symmetry principles in
      Physics
    - Actually the paper didn't say "symmetry". Noether focused
      on _transformations_ that leave the _action_ for some
      dynamical system invariant. (an exercise in the calculus of
      variations)
    - Normally though, transformations that leave the action
      invariant, also happen to be dynamical symmetries.
        - ie, they take solutions of the Euler-Lagrange equations
          into solutions of Euler-Lagrange

### Bohm - Quantum Theory - Trajectories
    - Consider a function thats 0 whenever the independent
      variable is rational, but 1 when its irrational. This
      function is completely discontinuous.
    - In physics, no attention is paid to such functions, because
      its tacitly assumed all functions describing the motion of
      actual material particles are continuous and
      differentiable.
    - This was done, mainly because it seems the most natural
      thing to do. But why does continuous motion seem so natural
      to us?
    - Actually many ancient Greeks were unable to graps the idea
      of continuous motion.
    - Eg Zeons paradox of an arrow in flight. Since each instant
      of time, the arrow is occupying a definite position, it
      cant at the same time be moving. Zeno concluded motion is
      somehow illusory. Many Greek philosophers were unable to
      convice themselves continous motion is really such
      a natural thing.
    - Between ancient times and now, our ideas of continous
      motion developed through experience with planetary orbits,
      gun trajectories, etc and the associated theory of
      differential calculus handling them.
    - After studying such things for a while, the basic ideas are
      gradually taken for granted.
    - But the only way to know if the motion of particles can
      actually be described by _functions with derivatives_  is
      to test this assumption by experiment.
    - ie, the classical idea a particle has a trajectory for
      which the derivative can be defined at each point is based
      only on empiricial evidence.
    - Since the days of Newton, the great success of classical
      theory provided strong empirical evidence and it seemed
      certain and in the nature of things that a continuous
      trajectory was the only kind that real matter could follow.
    - Yet by pure logic, there not reason to choose a continuous
      over a discountinuous trajectory.
    - Its possible that `∆x/∆t` approaches a limit for a while as
      `∆t` is decreased, then cease to approach a limit as `∆t`
      is made smaller still.
    - Consider an experiment measuring `∆x` for a real object,
      using a smaller and smaller `∆t`. For a while this gives
      increasingly accurate info about the velocity. But
      eventually we reach intervals of time so small that Browian
      motion becomes important and `∆x/∆t` ceases to approach
      a definite limit(!)
    - We could argue this could be avoided by treating the motion
      of individual molecules. But QM experiments have shown that
      if `∆t` is made too small, this effort will also fail.
    - We conclude that in a very accurate description, the
      concept of a continuous trajectory doesnt apply to the
      motion of real particles.

### Reciprocal basis vectors
    - More info: ./spacetime/spacetime-tips.txt
    - From this basis, you can create a brand new set of basis
      vectors.  They are called "RECIPROCAL" basis vectors.  The
      new set of "reciprocal" vectors are found by taking the
      cross product of any two original basis vectors and
      dividing by the scalar triple product.
    - original vectors:   `a_i`
    - reciprocal vectors: `b_i`
    - If you take the dot product between `a_i` and `b_j` you get
      a Kronecker delta.


### Dyson Advice
    - Roughly speaking, unfashionable mathematics consists of
      those parts the mandarins of Bourbaki declared not to be
      mathematics. A number of very beautiful mathematical
      discoveries fall into this category.
    - To be mathematics according to Bourbaki, an idea should be
      general, abstract, coherent, and connected by clear logical
      relationships with the rest of mathematics.
    - Excluded from mathematics are particular facts, concrete
      objects which just happen to exist for no identifiable
      reason, things which a mathematician would call accidental
      or sporadic.
    - Unfashionable mathematics is concerned with things of
      accidental beauty, special functions, particular number
      fields, exceptional algebras, sporadic finite groups.
    - It is among these unorganized and undisciplined parts of
      mathematics that I would advise you to look for the next
      revolution in physics.
    - They have a quality of strangeness, of unexpectedness. They
      do not fit easily into the smooth logical structures of
      Bourbaki.


### Pauli spin matrices = quaternions
    - So the story of gauge fields is full of ironies.
      A fashionable idea, invented for a purpose which turns out
      to be ephemeral, survives a long period of obscurity and
      emerges finally as a cornerstone of physics. Such ironies
      are not unusual in the history of mathematical physics.
    - Hamilton's invention of quaternions, hailed as a panacea
      for the problems of 19th century physics, was discarded as
      useless at the turn of the century, rejuvenated in the form
      of spin matrices in the QM of the 1920s, and
      has now ascended in glory to the quark field theories of
      the 1980s.
    - Gauss's invention of differential geometry, originating as
      a by-product of his work on the practical problems of
      geodesy and map-making, was transformed into a new world of
      abstract generality by the genius of Riemann, emerging 50
      years later as the conceptual basis of Einstein's theory of
      gravitation.

### GR is really about bending "time"???
    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/papers/VPython-Animations-for-GR-2019.pdf
    - Mathematically speaking, when replacing the above mentioned
      embedding diagram by a cone, we are approximating a square
      root function with a linear one. Surprisingly, even this
      first approximation enables us to show some aspects of
      curved space, namely the bending of straight trajectories
      when spatial curvature is introduced (for more details see
      the mentioned references).
    - However, all these uses have the same conceptual problems.
      First of all, they are based on the embedding diagram that
      ignores time curvature. General relativity is a theory of
      spacetime curvature and for most cases, including planetary
      orbits, curvature in time has a greater effect than space
      curvature. For this reason, even in the exact computer
      models, which we will see later, we can never get a stable
      orbit when dealing only with spatial curvature.
    - One may be, for example, tempted to try to use our
      embedding diagram to motivate the 2 nd Kepler’s law of
      planetary motion but, as was just said, stable orbits
      cannot be explained by spatial curvature only. This is the
      biggest downside of our use of this simplest of all
      embedding diagrams. There exist of course embedding
      diagrams including time (such as in [10]) but these are
      hardly suitable for beginner learners, such as
      upper-secondary school students, and are not (in the
      author’s experience) usually part of even undergraduate
      university courses in GR.

### Think evolutionary Physics, rather than like Plato
    - Accept that the standard model parameters are fine tuned
      and then ask:
        - “What dynamical mechanism tuned them?
        - (a process acting in the early universe, or perhaps
          before the big bang)
    - ... think like Darwin rather than Plato.
        - Faced with a diversity of species, Plato asked for
          absolute, timeless principles, that explained why
          these, and only these species, exist.
    - Darwin’s insight was that there are no such absolute
      principles. What we see in the biosphere is the result of
      a dynamical process: natural selection, acting over
      billions of years, that could have turned out differently.
    - But this dynamics is simple, profound and leads to many
      insights.

    - In fact, DM constitutes about 1/4 of our Universe. But its
      composition remains unknown. Ten or 15 years ago the
      general belief was that DM is built of LSP { the lightest
      supersymmetric particles. One of the less popular
      alternatives which was under consideration is the axion
      cloud around each galaxy. At present, one can find
      a spectrum of other hypotheses in the literature. Frankly,
      none of the newcomers seem aesthetically appealing. Nor are
      they motivated. Thankfully there is still hope that DM
      could be experimentally detected and studied. One should
      not forget, however, of a scenario in which DM components
      interact with us _only gravitationally_. This scenario is
      not ruled out, and if it is realized in nature, the DM
      structure will remain unknown in the near future.
    - The theme of supersymmetry (SUSY) which is so conspicuous
      in the Map (Fig. 3) redefined itself. To my mind, it is no
      longer dominated by SUSY phenomenology. The second face of
      SUSY { supersymmetry as a tool for exploring gauge dynamics
      at strong coupling { which started emerging in the early
      1980s is taking precedence over phenomenology. It is
      unclear for how long, though. A decisive breakthrough in
      this direction occurred in 1994, with the discovery of the
      Seiberg duality and the Seiberg-Witten solution of
      N = 2 supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory slightly deformed by
      a mass term of the adjoint chiral superfield [11].
    - This remarkable achievemnt to a large extent feeds
      continuous advances of two branches at the top of the

      right-hand side and the other labeled \QFT/SC" on the

### Original Conception of the "Photon"
    - Photon: New light on an old name. Helge Kragh
    - The brilliant chemist Lewis also had a deep interest in
      theoretical physics, though he preferred to follow his own,
      heterodox ideas rather than the mainstream. In 1925 he
      became interested in the conceptual problems of radiation
      theory and attempted to solve them by postulating that time
      is symmetric (as a fundamental principle).
    - According to his picture of the radiation process, emission
      and absorption of light occurred in complete symmetry. “An
      atom never emits light except to another atom,” he wrote.
    - “It is as absurd to think of light emitted by one atom
      regardless of the existence of a receiving atom as it would
      be to think of an atom absorbing light without the
      existence of light being absorbed” [Lewis 1926a, p. 24;
      Stuewer 1975b].
    - The emission process would be the exact reverse of the
      absorption process, therefore the two separate processes
      could be condensed into a single one: _transmission_.
    - It was in this context he proposed in a paper in Nature
      (1926) that instead of the light quantum, one should
      consider “a new kind of atom” or what he called a “photon”
      as the carrier of light [Lewis 1926b].
    - Contrary to Einstein’s light quantum, Lewis’ photon was
      a conserved quantity in the sense that in an isolated
      system the total number of photons would remain constant.
    - The photon was uncreatable and indestructible, unlike the
      modern photon but much like John Dalton’s immutable atoms
      of the past:
        - “We might as well attempt to introduce a new planet
          into the solar system, or to annihilate one already in
          existence, as to create or destroy a particle of
          hydrogen” [Dalton 1808, p. 212].
    - Replace “particle of hydrogen” with “photon,” and you have
      Lewis’ claim. Further arguing that “one, and only one,
      photon is lost in each elementary radiation process,” Lewis
      [1926b, p. 875] wrotej
        > It would seem inappropriate to speak of one of these
        > hypothetical entities as a particle of light, a light
        > quantum, or a light quant, if we are to assume that it
        > spends only a minute fraction of its existence as
        > a carrier of radiant energy, while the rest of the time
        > it remains as an important structural element within
        > the atom. It would also cause confusion to call it
        > merely a quantum, for … it will be necessary to
        > distinguish between the number of these entities
        > present in the atom and the so-called quantum number.
        > I therefore take the liberty of proposing for this
        > hypothetical new atom, which is not light but plays an
        > essential part in every process of radiation, the name
        > photon.
    - Lewis’ photon thus was very different from the corpuscle of
      radiant energy that Einstein had introduced.
    - It is misleading to write about “Einstein’s fundamental
      light-quantum, which Gilbert N. Lewis had christened the
      ‘photon’ in October 1926” [Mehra and Rechenberg 2000, p.
      241].

### Solvay
    - $MY_BOOKS/Physics/QM/reconsidering-solvay.pdf
    - In 1923 DeBroglie made one of the most astounding
      predictions in theoretical physics: that material bodies
      would exhibit the wave-like phenomena of diffraction and
      interference upon passing through sufficiently narrow slits
    - DeBroglies prediction of the deflection of electron paths
      by narrow slits was made from a fundamental reappraisal of
      the nature of forces and dynamics. (Like Einsteins
      prediction of the deflection of light by gravity)
    - DeBroglie proposed that Newtons first law of motion be
      abandoned, and replaced by a new postulate, according to
      which a freely moving body follows sa trajectory ortogonal
      to surfaces of equal phase of an associated guiding wave.
    - This "Pilot Wave Theory" was a new approach to the _theory
      of motion_, as radical as Einsteins of the trajectories of
      falling bodies as geodesics of a curved spacetime.
    - DeBroglies phd thesis was the creation of a new form of
      dynamics where classical laws (Newtonian, Einsteinian)
      where particle _velocities_ are determined by guiding
      waves, in a manner that unifies the _variational_
      principles of Maupertuis and Fermat. (!!)
    - DeBroglie proposed that Newton's first law of motion should
      be abandoned, and replaced by a new principle that unified
      Maupertuis' variational principle for mechanics with
      Fermat's variational principle for optics.
    - The result was a new form of dynamics in which the velocity
      `ṽ` of a particle is determined by the gradient of the
      phase `φ` of an accompanying wave --- in contrast with
      classical mechanics, where accelerations are determined by
      forces.
    - DeBroglie whole numbers:
            > .... the determination of the stable motions of
            > electrons in the atom involves WHOLE NUMBERS, and
            > so far the only phenomena of whole numbers in
            > physics are interference and eigenvibrations.
            > That suggested the idea to me that electrons
            > themselves could not be represented as simple
            > corpuscles either, but that a periodicity had also
            > to be assigned to them too.

### Hubbard: Describe 'Forces', not 'motions'
    - Still, the epicycle theory is involved and complicated;
      although the motions of the planets really are complicated.
      In fact, theres no simple description of the motions of the
      planets.
    - The main point of Newton's work was the realization that
      the _forces_ are simpler than the motions. There is no more
      forceful motivation for the theory of differential
      equations than this.
    - And the modus operandi introduced by Newton is still
      largely respected: all basic physical laws are stated as
      differential equations, whether Maxwell's equations,
      Schrodinger's, or Einstein's GR.
    - The philosophy of _forces being simpler than motions_ is
      very general, and when you wish to model a real system,
      you'll want to describe the forces, and derive the motions
      from them; rather than describing the motions _directly_.

    - For instance, in "The Stability of a Rotating Liquid Drop"

### Lewis GR - gradients (1-forms) and basis vectors
    - The above definition of vectors involved specifying basis
      vectors, which are in essence directions along coordinate
      lines - ∂/∂x, ∂/∂r etc.
    - This gives sense to the idea that a vector is a quantity
      'with magnitude and direction'.
    - But theres another type of 'vector' quantity in elementary
      calculus, which is the gradient of a (scalar) function. For
      example the function f (x, y, z) has a gradient
        - ∆f = ∂f/∂x i + ∂f/∂y j + ∂f/∂z k
    - In this case the gradients are proportional to the vectors
      `er`, `eθ`, `e` with `eθ`, `e` spanning the tangent space
      to `S2`.
    - However, this isnt typical, since a spherical surface in
      a flat 3-dimensional space has a very high degree of
      symmetry.
    - Conceptually, gradients and basis vectors are _distinct_.
    - basis vectors are associated with coordinate lines, but
      gradients with 'lines of steepest descent' from one surface
      to another, as sketched in Fig. 3.5.
    - This difference may be shown up by considering
      a non-orthogonal coordinate system in R3; define u, v and
      w by x ¼ u þ v; y ¼ u  v; z ¼ 2 u v þ w with inverse
      u ¼ 1=2ðx þ yÞ; v ¼ 1=2ðx  yÞ; w ¼ z  1=2ðx2  y2Þ:


### Hassani
   - Coordinates are "functions" that specify points of a space.
   - The smallest number of these functions necessary to specify
     a point is called the dimension of that space.
    - Eg. a point of a plane is specified by 2 numbers, and as
      the point moves in the plane, the 2 numbers change. ie the
      coordinates are functions of the position of the point.
    - If we call the point `P`, we can write the coordinate
      functions of `P` as `(f(P), g(P))`. Each _pair_ of such
      functions is called a coordinate system. (Think of `f` and
      `g` as a rule by which a unique number is assigned to each
      point `P`)
    - Two coordinate systems used for a plane are:
        - Cartesian: `(x(P), y(P))`
        - Polar:     `(r(P), θ(P))`
        - $MY_IMAGES/physics/coordinate-systems.jpg
        - As shown in the figure, "function" `x` is defined as
          giving the distance from `P` to the vertical axis.
        - And `θ` is the function giving the angle that the line
          `OP` makes with a given fiducial (usually horizontal)
          line.
        - Note: the origin `O` and the fidicual line are
          completely arbitrary.
    - Now listen to this!
        - In practice, we drop the argument `P` and write `(x, y)`
          and `(r, θ)`
        - Wow! So thats the true definition of a coordinate
          system!

### Div, Grad Curl are Dead
    - We'll cover 2 main ideas. One is the evolution in calculus
      away from the concept of a _derivative_ towards the concept
      of the _differential_. This makes single and multi-variable
      calculus fit smoothly together.
    - The second is to use both vectors and their duals, the
      differential forms. (So a "form" is a dual to a vector!)
        - This allows calculus to be applied in spaces with no
          natural Euclidean metric such as thermodynamics or
          relativity, and without the need to be in 3 dimensions.
        - Remarkably, such a generalisation turns out to be
          computationally convenient even in 3D.
    - I'll start with some basic facts on linear algebra,
      multilinear algebra, affine algebra and multi-affine
      algebra. Actually I would rather call this _linear
      geometry_ but I follow historical usage here.
    - This is to repair the omissions of a traditional course
      which is typically about matrix multiplication.
    - I realised these omissions when I emailed local
      mathematicians:
        - How do you guys treat dual spaces in linear algebra?
        - Answer: We don't.
    - Associated with any linear space is another linear space
      called its dual.
        - Elements of the dual space are operators which map
          elements of the given linear space into the real
          numbers, in a way that _preserves the linear
          structure_.
            - preserve linearity: ie, the map of the sum of
              2 vectors, is the sum of the maps.
        - Me: So for a dual space, you need an operator and
          a map??? No! The operator _is_ the map? Nov 2020 YES!
        - The most familiar duality is between row and column
          vectors, with the map given by matrix multiplication.
        - If your linear space is a _shopping cart_ full of
          groceries, then the check-out clerk is a linear
          operator on that space. Me: so the clerk "lives" in the
          dual space? yes
        - Also an example with continuous functions on the unit
          interval. Should get a screen grab of it...

### Reddit: Div, Grad Curl are Dead
    - Good stuff, but very introductory. I'm searching for more
      on differential geometry; but I already know Geometric
      Algebra, which makes many of the same insights.
    - From what I can see here, the two have a strong common
      basis, and may even be the same thing.
    - Both are based on the fact you can define addition and
      multiplication to be closed over vectors, as long as you
      allow your notation to express all the results. I like the
      icons Burke used to express the different "types" of
      vectors; as far as I can see, that's a unique contribution.
        - I'm going to explore this more on arXiv.org.

    - The question "why differential forms?" has good answers
      both mathematically (from the perspective of global
      analysis and differential geometry) and physically (from
      the perspective of classical mechanics, to start).

### Burke
    - Here we'll turn the infinitesimals seen in physics into the
      appropriate geometric objects, usually either _rates_
      (tangent vectors) or _gradients_ (differential forms). The
      distinction between these is lost in the metric-blinded
      symbol pushing of tensor calculus.
    - Nor will the funny deltas of the calculus of variations
      with their ad-hoc rules of manipulation be found here.
    - The idea behind calculus: The aim of calculus on manifolds
      is to extend the familiar ideas of calculus to sets other
      than Euclidean space. The key idea is simple: its the
      technology of linear approximations to non-linear objects:
      straight lines as linear approximations to curves, planes
      for surfaces etc.
    - This idea underlies all the calculus on manifolds. We'll
      find it simpler to concentrate on the linear approximation
      itself, e.g. the tangent line, rather than its
      representation, the slope of that tangent line.
        - We'll alternate between discussion of an objects to
          discussion of its representation. Sometimes its useful
          to make this distinction, sometimes not.

### Almost all about waves
    - Energy, momentum and velocity are important properties of
      waves. Another truly astounding property that many waves
      exhibit is _linearity_.
    - We find it easy to define the energy, momentum and velocity
      only in terms of a particular, simple sort of wave:
      a sinusoidal wave
    - Fortunately, other sorts of waves can be represented as
      sums of sinusoidal waves
    - The phase velocity of the wave `v = ω / k`. As the wave
      moves, the whole sinusoidal pattern moves to the right with
      a velocity `v`. If we also move to the right with
      a velocity `v` we see the sinusoidal pattern of the wave as
      a _stationary_ ripple.

### Preptorial review of Curry
    - Dynamics teachers use 4 "channels" for different problems:
        - ANALYTIC:     formulas
        - QUALITATIVE:  geometric and graphic
        - NUMERIC:      algorithmic starting with "seed" guesses
        - STOCHASTIC:
            - mostly research applications where combinatorics
              get so hairy they are handled as random or chaotic.
            - (Even some oscillators like pendulums are nonlinear
              because they have a sin function, or when they
              "loop over" the top of the pendulum when enough
              force is applied. How much? That's where they
              become nonlinear!).

    - Hilary Putnam <hilary.putnam@gmail.com>
    - to David Albert, Goldstein, Maudlin, Tumulka
        - Dear David.
        - I recall that you have said more than once that I give
          too much weight to the failure of Lorentz invariance as
          an objection to Bohmian mechanics (BM).
        - If SR were the state of the art description of
          space-time, the fact that the violations of Lorentz
          invariance in BM are undetectable according to BM
          itself, might be a way of mitigating, if not wholly
          disposing of, that objection.
        - But SR is not the state of the art description;
        - the best description we have is GR (pending a theory
          that reconciles GR with QM).
        - The standard model in cosmology is soaked with
          GR-expansion, inflation, black holes, etc., etc; all GR
          phenomena.
        - And GR implies that while SR is wrong globally, it is
          right locally; as we all know, in infinitesimal
          regions, the space-time is MInkowskian.
        - And BM is wedded to Euclidean space-time. It derived,
          after all, from De Broglie's attempt to see the
          psi-function as the description of a _wave in flat 3+1
          space-time_.

### Mathematical dictionary (ERIC WEINSTEIN)
    -  In some ways we've been making amazing progress for 40
       years- e.g. the _MATHEMATICS OF FIELD THEORY_, which is
       the underlying geometric structure for both particle
       theory and general relativity.

    - a "dictionary" has opened up which imports the best
      insights from physics into differential geometry and from
      differential geometry back into physics.

    - The problem is we really wanted to _quantize_ the geometry
      of GR but we've actually ended up _geometrizing the
      quantum_.

    - Is Einstein, in fact, wrong to slip in _space-time_ on the
      ground floor of the physics shared by both QFT and GR?

    - He has a beautiful quote about his equation which can be
      viewed as a mansion with two wings:
        - one made from fine _marble_ (geometry space-time)
        - the other from _cheap wood_ (matter & stress energy tensor)

### Whitaker: Einstein's Struggles with Quantum Theory
    - Mach chose to define mass through Newton's 3rd law; in an
      action-reaction pair, `m1/m2` is defined as being equal to
      `a2/a1`
    - ie. defined directly from measurable quantities. (Barbour
      said this too)

    - In retrospect we realise that analysing translational
      motion using absolute space is complicated by an infinity
      of inertial frames, but theres no such problem for
      rotational motion; Newton took full advantage of this fact.

    - Mach responded with spirit. First he said that the lack of
      _effect of relative motion_ between water and vessel may be
      a result of the _limited thickness_ of the bucket walls;
      nobody knows, he said, what might happen if the walls were
      several miles thick. (ie there might be a gravitational tug
      from the mass of thick bucket walls. A bit like frame
      dragging?)

    - Secondly he pointed out that the motion of the water that
      produces the centrifugal effect is by no means absolute; it
      is a relative motion: relative in fact to the fixed stars.

    - Challenging Newton, Mach inquired: What'd heppen if the
      water were at rest relative to Newton's absolute space, and
      the stars rotated around the bucket? Mach affirms that
      Ptolemy and Copernicus are equally correct: Copernicus was
      preferred only because his ideas were more simple / useful.

      the fundamentals than Mach's Principle:
        * Would you feel centrifugal forces in an empty universe?
        * Does the law of inertia mean anything in an empty
          universe?
    - To explain the origin of accelerations, he would need to
      create a theory of gravitation. Furthermore, because free
      fall abolishes gravity in an elevator, _the origin of
      inertia cannot be found in the interaction with nearby
      bodies such as Earth_.
    - While still formulating GR, Einstein calculated that the
      gravitational field of a shell of matter rotating around
      a gyroscope (picture a gyro inside a spinning, hollow
      Earth) would displace the gyro's axis relative to the fixed
      stars.
    - The gravitational field literally drags around the gyro's
      "inertial compass."
    - The question of how the distant reaches of the universe
      give marching orders to a gyroscope spinning on my desk is
      an altogether trickier issue.
    - In calculating the effects of curved space around the Sun,
      Einstein needed to assume that at large distances from the
      Sun, spacetime becomes flat - and absolute.
    - In other words, he had to impose "_boundary conditions_" at
      infinity (by fiat) to complete his solution. GR of itself,
      did not entirely determine the precession of Mercury's
      orbit.
    - Godel assumed the same uniform distribution of matter as
      Einstein did in his first cosmological model of 1917, but
      Godel's solution exhibited fundamentally different
      behavior.
    - That discrepancy directly contradicted Einstein's premise
      that the universe's matter distribution should _uniquely_
      determine gyroscopic behavior.
    - More recently, Christoph Schmid claims to have vindicated
      both Einstein and Mach.
    - To most contemporary physicists, Mach's Principle is not
      merely redundant, it is forgotten entirely. Yet the mystery
      is never far away. To this day, we do not truly understand
      how the distant universe gives gyroscopes their marching
      orders.
    - But replace the quaint-sounding words "Mach's Principle"
      with "Why does a gyroscope point in a direction fixed
      relative to distant quasars?" and one stands face to face
      with one of the most striking questions nature presents to
      us.
        - HN comments
        - We can predict the behavior of a gyro but there is
          still a fundamental question of what a gyro is
          _relative to_ since GR tells us there is no universal
          reference frame.
        - Are gyroscopes entirely local or does the gravity of
          distant galaxies present a sort of _universal
          reference_ frame? Or is it something else entirely?




### Wald: review of Maudlin
    - There is no justification for treating clocks and rods
      asymmetrically: if its appropriate to say physical rods
      undergo a physical Lorentz contraction, then it must be
      equally appropriate to say physical clocks undergo
      a "physical time dilation." And that concession leads back
      to the usual discussions of the twin paradox that Maudlin
      demolishes in chapter 4.

    - It would be better if Maudlin consistently stuck to the
      view that all of the structure of space and time in SR is
      described by:
        - the topological and differential properties of events
        - together with the spacetime metric.

    - My expectation is that a perceptive reader will feel highly
      enlightened by the clear explanations in chapter 4, which
      uses clocks as _idealized_ objects, but could feel uneasy
      after chapter 5, where rods are introduced only as
      _physical_ objects.

### newton_rotation.txt
    - Newton thought one type of absolute motion could be
      identified: _rotational motion_!
    - and argued for this using his "rotating bucket"  !!!!

    - On the other hand, Leibniz subscribed to the 17th century
      'equipollence of hypotheses', whereby all motion is
      relative to whatever body (or system of bodies) is
      hypothesized to be at rest, and the phenomena and laws of
      physics are invariant under change of hypothesis.

    - Thus we have Newton the absolutist, holding that absolute
      _rotational_ motions define a preferred frame of reference
      for _all_ motion: absolute space;
        - and Leibniz the relativist, holding that all motion is
          relative to whatever body is taken to be at rest,
          places to be relative in the same way, and space to be
          any of the relative spaces so determined

    - The usual view is Newton was almost right about the
      relativity of motion and the nature of space. His mechanics
      embodies an equivalence of inertial frames; ie the laws of
      Newtonian physics are the same in all inertial reference
      frames, or invariant under a transformation from one frame
      to another. This fact is duly recognized in Corollary V to
      his Laws of Motion:

        > The motions of bodies included in a given space are the
        > same among themselves, whether that space is at rest,
        > or moves uniformly forward in a straight line without
        > any circular motion. (Newton [1726], p. 63; my
        > translation)

    - Notoriously however, Newton went on to claim that one of
      these spaces is absolutely at _rest_: absolute space. His
      famous argument of the rotating bucket is usually
      interpreted as an attempt to provide a criterion (_at least
      for circular motion_) for absolute motion or rest.

    - From a modern perspective, Newton's intuitions are on
      track. For the absoluteness of circular motion - as opposed
      to linearly accelerated motion - is _preserved not only in
      SR_, but also in GR, in spite of Einstein's expectations;
      a point not always appreciated.

### Drawning the line between kinematics and dynamics in SR
    - Michel Janssen
    - ... Earman's book also took care of the centuries-old
      objection that absolute motion is incompatible with
      relationism by clearly separating the absolute/relative
      _motion_ distinction from the substantival/relational
      _ontology_ distinction. (interesting!!)

    - Brown (2005) writes: The real issue is... whether physical
      geometry... when it is absolute and immune to perturbation
      as in Newtonian and Minkowskian spacetime... offers
      a causal explanation of anything'' (p. 26).

    - I claim that Minkowski spacetime explains Lorentz
      invariance. For this to be a causal explanation, Minkowski
      spacetime would have to be a substance with causal
      efficacy. Like Brown, I reject this view (Janssen, 2002b,
      p. 468).
    - As I hope to make clear, Minkowski spacetime explains by
      identifying the kinematical nature (rather than the cause)
      of the relevant phenomena.

    - SR as a physical theory is _agnostic_ about the ontology of
      spacetime. I argue that the orthodox version of this
      physical theory is preferable to the alternative proposed
      by Brown because it provides better guidance for further
      research.

    - Because my argument is ultimately about such methodological
      issues and not ontology, it had better be independent of
      whether one is a relationist or a substantivalist about
      Minkowski spacetime (Balashov & Janssen, 2003, p. 341, note
      11). The challenge in this case is to produce an argument
      that works for the relationist. The substantivalist can
      always make that same argument work by reifying the
      relevant relations. I can thus join the debate with Brown
      on his own relationist turf without compromising the focus
      on methodological issues.

### Gravitataion from Newton to Einstein
    - Pierre Fleury
    - https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.07287
    - notes of a 30-hour review course on gravitation, delivered
      January 2018, 2019 at (AIMS-Cameroon).
    - Its main goal is to propose a big picture of gravitation,
      where Einstein's relativity arises as a natural increment
      to Newton's theory.
    - It is designed for BSc/MSc students who dont necessarily
      have prior knowledge of relativity.
    - The first chapter is a mere reformulation of known
      concepts.
    - The second chapter then introduces SR and GR at the same
      time, while the third chapter explores concrete
      manifestations of relativistic gravitation, notably
      gravitational waves and black holes.
    - Each section corresponds, on average, to a 2-hour
      black-board lecture. The numerous exercises must be
      considered part of the course itself; they are intended to
      stimulate active reading.

### What is a lie group
    - from a review of James curry
    - It also has 3 chapters devoted to Lie Groups and Lie
      Algebras. Most elementary treatments describe Lie Groups as
      "Groups of Matrices." Yes, thats how theyre often realized,
      but that misses the point.
    - A Lie group is a group which is _also a manifold_.
    - And a Lie algebra is the set of all left invariant vector
      fields on the Lie group
    - They are connected by the _exponential map_, which sends
      a vector in the Lie algebra to a point
        - along a path tangent to that vector
        - down in the group.

### Jim Curry random comments
    - With Woodhouse, you easily notice that a geodesic is just
      the solution to Lagrange's equation with a _null
      potential_.
    - That makes sense physically. We can "see" it or "feel" it:
      if a particle isn't acted on by a force, it goes as
      straight as it can.
    - Woodhouse makes no attempt to sidestep the mathematics, and
      that is appropriate. There's no understanding of relativity
      apart from the mathematics. So grin and embrace it.
    - Geometric algebra could be considered the "correct"
      generalization of linear algebra.
    - By considering directed _line_ segments, university courses
      achieve miracles.
    - Although viewed by some students as just an unpleasant
      bookkeeping systems, linear algebra derives its power from
      allowing the _algebraic manipulation_ of sophisticated
      aggregate objects: namely vectors.
    - The benefits are not just computational, but offer a more
      powerful and unified viewpoint for the student (although
      more abstract).
    - Geometric algebra is this and more. By extending the
      consideration of directed line segments to include directed
      _plane_ segments, directed elements of 3-space, etc, an
      extremely elegant mathematical tool arises.
    - It allows a deeper, more concise treatment of basically all
      of modern differential geometry. Its applications in
      physics are at once simplifications of ordinary matrix
      treatments and occasions of greater insight.
    - "Temperature is a very exciting idea you must learn about
      in statistical mechanics. Some people say that temperature
      is the average energy. That is a lie." (!!)
        - (He goes on to allude to the definition of temperature
          in terms of entropy, without using the term "entropy".)
          "It [thermodynamics] is very good stuff. Learn it
          soon!"

### Jimy Curry: linear transformations
    - A linear transformation can seem abstract, at least at
      first. Only later, when the _polar decomposition_ is
      encountered (sometimes omitted) does it make more sense.

    - A linear transformation can be compounded as:
        - a (possible) reflection,
        - a shearing motion,
        - and a rotation.

    - So, it really does have a geometric underpinning. If bases
      are _fixed_, in both the {`domain`} and {`range`} vector
      space of the linear transformation, then it is usually
      represented as a matrix - just a table of numbers. These
      can be indexed nicely to keep the "bookkeeping" straight.

    - Tensors are _multi-linear mappings of spaces_ compounded
      from different vector spaces. Again, these can be addressed
      abstractly, or as tables of numbers.



### british_library.txt
    - ### Darrigol - Physics and necessity
        - In Newtons opinion, mechanics _preceeded_ geometry
          because it constructs figures that are the basis of
          geometrical reasoning.
            > Geometry is founded in mechanical pratice and is
            > that part of Universal Mechanics which accurately
            > proposes and demonstrates the art of measuring.
        - Newton indeed invented his differential geometry(!?) in
          a mechancial context.

    - ### Leibniz
        - introduced a sharp distinction between necessary and
          contingent truths:
            - _neccessary_: can be reduced to an identity through
              analysis; ie, their truth is a logical consequence
              of the definition of the terms they contain. (like
              Kants a priori?)

            - _contingent_: can only be the consequence of
              another truth. The reduction to identity is still
              possible, but only through an infinite chain of
              antecedents accessible to God alone. For humans,
              contingent truths can only be known a posteriori,
              through experience, unless they can be traced to
              a natural expression of Gods wisdom.

        - Because there's two types of truths, there are 2 basic
          principles of reasoning:

            1. principle of contradiction -> where necessary
               truths are derived

            2. principle of sufficient reason -> which defines
               contingent truths (... "that a reason can be given
               for every truth, or, as is commonly said, that
               nothing happens without a cause")

    - ### Euler's "Mechanica"
        - Like Newton, he assumed absolute space as a fictitious
          rigid body of infinite extension

        - He then derived the principle of intertia from the
          principle of _sufficient reason_.

        - He argued that a body originally at rest in empty space
          could not start moving because it had no _reason_ to
          move one way or the other.

        - And he argued a body originally moving in space had to
          conserve its _direction_ of motion since it had no more
          reason to be deflected one way than the other. And its
          _velocity_ modulus could not change since it had no
          more reason to decrease than to increase.

        - Euler regarded the parallelogram of forces as
          a necessary law (agreed with Bernoulli's derivation)


### Principle of least action
    - "Principle of Least Potential": Express a law of mechanics
      by the condition that an extremum of a certain quantity can
      be traced to the principle that a connected system of
      weights is in equilibrium if and only if the height of its
      centre of gravity is a _minimum_

    - This principle has roots in the Aristotelian notion of
      a natural motion toward the centre of the universe (was
      exploited by Renaissance mechanicians like da Vinci and
      Torricelli generalised it to indifferent equilibrium)

      Darrigol: The reasoning behind the "principle of least
      potential" is merely intuitive, and a naive analogy leads
      from the principle of least potential to the principle of
      least action. We will later see whether, despite these
      shortcommings, something can be saved of Eulers
      rationalism.


### D'Alembert
    - He rejeted Descartes materiality of pure extension, and
      defined motion as Newton and Euler did: the displacement of
      bodies in absolute space.
    - He did not reduce action-at-a-distance to contact action
      through a medium. Like Newton he contented himself with
      a _mathematical_ expression of this action.
    - He didnt require the conservation of motion; instead the
      _destruction_ of motion was a central concept of his
      dynamics.
    - He was proud of banishing the causal relation between force
      and acceleration from his mechanics.
    - In his system, the formula `f = ma` is only a definition of
      force; the only one available since the true cause of the
      motion is forever _unknown_. Force has no independent
      existence.

### dimension-analysis.txt
    - The origins of dimensional analysis have been disputed by
      historians. [^1] [^2]

    - Joseph Fourier is credited with having made important
      contributions [^3] based on the idea that physical laws
      like `F = ma` should be independent of the units employed
      to measure the physical variables.

    - This led to the conclusion that meaningful laws must be
      homogeneous equations in their various units of
      measurement, a result which was eventually formalized in
      the _Buckingham pi theorem_.

    - Maxwell played a major role in establishing modern use of
      dimensional analysis by distinguishing mass, length, and
      time as fundamental units, while referring to other units
      as derived.[^4]

    - though Maxwell defined length, time and mass to be "the
      three fundamental units", he  also noted that
      _gravitational mass can be derived from length and time_ by
      assuming a form of Newton's law of Gravitation in which the
      gravitational constant `G` is taken as unity, giving
        - `M = L^{3} T^{-2} `

    - Assuming a form of Coulomb's law in which Coulomb's
      constant `k_e` is unity, Maxwell then determined the
      dimensions of an electrostatic unit of charge as: [^6]
        - `\frac{ L^{3/2} M^{1/2} } { T^{-1} } `

    - which, after substituting his $M = L^3 T^{-2}$ equation for
      mass, results in charge having the _same dimensions as
      mass_: $$ Q = L^{3} T^{-2} $$

    - Dimensional analysis is also used to derive _relationships_
      between the physical quantities involved in a particular
      phenomenon one wishes to understand.

    - It was used for the first time in this way in 1872 by
      Rayleigh, to understand why the sky is blue. Rayleigh first
      published the technique in his 1877 book 'The Theory of
      Sound'.

    - The original meaning of the word _dimension_, in Fourier's
      'Theorie de la Chaleur', was the numerical value of the
      _exponents_ of the base units.

    - For example, acceleration had the dimension `1` with
      respect to the unit of length, and the dimension `-2` with
      respect to the unit of time.

    - This was slightly changed by Maxwell, who said the
      dimensions of acceleration are `L T^{-2}`, instead of just
      the exponents.

    - [^1]: [Historico-critical review of dimensional analysis](http://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0016003271901608)
    - [^2]: [The origin of dimensinal analysis](http://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0016003281904750)

### energy_conserve_uni.txt
    - Is energy conserved in an expanding universe? Thats
      a tricky question, depending on what you mean by "energy."

    - Usually we assign energy to different components of the
      universe (radiation, matter, dark energy), not including
      gravity itself. In that case the total energy, given by
      adding the energy density in each component, is certainly
      not conserved.

    - The most dramatic example occurs with dark energy: the
      energy density (energy per unit volume) remains
      approximately constant, while the volume increases as the
      universe expands, so the total energy _increases_.

    - But even ordinary radiation exhibits similar behavior; the
      number of photons remains constant, while each individual
      photon loses energy as it redshifts, so the total energy in
      _radiation decreases_.
        - (A decrease in energy is just as much a violation of
          energy conservation as an increase)

    - In a sense, the energy in "stuff" is being transferred to
      the energy of the gravitational field, as manifested in the
      expansion of the universe. But there is no exact definition
      of "the energy of the gravitational field," so this
      explanation is imperfect.

    - Nevertheless, although energy is not really conserved in an
      expanding universe, there is a very strict rule that is
      obeyed by the total energy, which reduces to perfect
      conservation when the expansion rate goes to zero; the
      _expansion_ changes the rules, but that doesn't mean that
      anything goes.

### inter-molecular repulsion and attraction
    - Why causes repulsion?
    - Not electron-electron repulsion but the Pauli Exclusion!
    - Its a quantum mechanical effect. Very difficult to model
      because it depends on the molecule and the system.

    - The van der Waals equation of state tries to roughly mimic
      all the complexities but theres no universal equation of
      state.
    - The van der Waals equation is known as a "mean field"
      equation.

    - A viral expansion will all have coefficeints that depend on
      temperature divided by increasing power of `V`.

    - Van der Waals is a very special case of a viral expansion
      where all the coefficients are degenerated and put into one
      parameter. (which parameter??!)
    - Van der Waals thesis was about this. It was considered so
      important in the 19th century that Maxwell learnt Dutch
      just to be able to read it in the original.

    - But what causes the attraction?
    - Answer: The induced dipole effect.
        - also called the Van der Waals force.

    - Theres a dipole dipole interaction.

    - Now if the molecules were perfectly spherically symmetrical
      there would be no electric field outside. But these are
      actually dynamical entities and are changing all the time.
      Instantaneous fluctuations can cause a temporary dipole
      moment.

    - Between both dipole moments you get a potential energy that
      changes as `1/r^6`

    - However if the molecules have permanent electric dipole
      moments (like water) then this is not the answer.


### liquid_mercury.txt
    - According to SR, the apparent mass of an object increases
      as its velocity approaches the speed of light.

    - From Niels Bohr's theory of atomic structure we know that
      the _velocity_ of an electron is proportional to the atomic
      number of an element.

    - For light elements like hydrogen (atomic number 1) the
      velocity is insignificant compared to the speed of light so
      relativity can be essentially ignored. But for the 1s
      electron of mercury (atomic number 80) this effect becomes
      significant; the electron approaches about 58% of the speed
      of light, and its mass increases to 1.23 times its rest
      mass. Relativity has kicked in.

    - Since the radius of an electron orbit in the Bohr theory
      (orbital to be precise) goes inversely as the mass, this
      mass increase results in a 23% decrease in the orbital
      radius. This shrinkage makes a world of difference since it
      results in stronger attraction between the nucleus and the
      electrons, and this effect translates to the outermost 6s
      orbital as well as to other orbitals. The effect is
      compounded by the more diffuse 'd' and 'f' orbitals
      insufficiently shielding the 's' electrons.

    - Combined with the filled nature of the 6s orbital, the
      relativistic shrinkage makes mercury very reluctant indeed
      to share its outermost electrons and form strong bonds with
      other mercury atoms.

### long range order
    - solid,
        - long range order
        - inter-atomic spacing is repeated again and again
        - ie. go 100 times the basic _lattice_ spacing and
        - there'll be another atom sitting there

    - liquid
        - short range positional order,
        - no long range order

    - gas
        - no order at all,
        - nobody has an assigned location

### non-thermal radiation
    - If the emitted radiation does not depend on THE TEMPERATURE
      OF THE SOURCE, the radiation is known as 'non-thermal
      radiation'.
    - Some objects such as supernovas, pulsars, radio galaxies,
      Seyfert galaxies, BL Lacertae objects and GRBs produce
      photons that CANNOT be described as "blackbody radiation"
      or "thermal radiation."
    - These photons almost certainly were not made by electrons
      _changing their orbit_. This process is not as well
      understood by scientists, and what is known may be
      incomplete.

    - In astronomy, there are 3 common types of non-thermal
      radiation:

    1. _Synchrotron_ emission
        - generated by charged particles spiralling around
          magnetic field lines at relativistic speeds.
        - (it's a type of Bremsstrahlung radiation)

    2. _Compton_ scattering
        - where electrons are effectively pushed around by high
          energy photons, which scatter off the electron after
          imparting some of their energy.

    3. _Stimulated emission_
        - where electrons in a metastable state are prompted to
          decay to the ground state by a passing photon with the
          same energy as the difference between the two levels.

### Bremsstrahlung
    - EM radiation produced by the deceleration of a charged
      particle when deflected by another charged particle,
      typically an electron by an atomic nucleus.
    - Strictly speaking, bremsstrahlung refers to any radiation
      due to the _acceleration of a charged particle_, which
      includes synchrotron radiation;
    - however, it is frequently used in the more narrow sense of
      radiation from ELECTRONS STOPPING IN MATTER.

    - For me it's a dream that eventually number theory would
      make contact with physics, but I doubt it will be soon.

    - There are all kinds of areas where number theory formulas
      appear in physics, and these may be clues that the dream
      will come true one day.

    - But to really get me excited, somehow the number theory
      would have to enter the physics in a more structural way.
    - I'm not that interested in a specific formula that comes
      from a physics calculation in a more or less ad-hoc way.
      Number theory would have to be more integrated with the
      physics to get me excited, and I don t see that happening
      soon.

    - In my work, I concentrated on the geometric form of the
      Langlands correspondence because I could see the hope to
      really understand it in the context of the physics-based
      tools that were at hand. There might be something like that
      one day for the Langlands correspondence of number theory,
      but probably a lot is missing and we do not know what has
      to happen first.


### A dynamics with inequalities
    - Hard constraints are represented by inequalities. Hard
      constraints occur frequently in practical systems and their
      models; these hard constraints are often used in
      optimization since optima are frequently found at these
      hard limits.
    - In optimal control theory, this can be seen in the
      prevalence of "bang-bang" solutions: the "bang" represents
      a control at a hard limit.
    - In spite of this, hard limits are eschewed in most
      dynamical models. There are a number of reasons for this.
      One is the lack of a suitable or "nice" theory for such
      systems. Another is that numerical methods do not handle
      this situation well. A third is that it is often not clear
      what should happen in a differential equation when a hard
      limit is reached.

### Sabine
    - Dark Matter is... matter. But "matter" is not just
      a physicists way of saying "stuff" - it's a technical term.
    - Matter has specific behavior: its energy-density dilutes
      with the inverse volume.
    - The Energy-density of radiation, in contrast, dilutes
      faster than the inverse volume, because the wavelengths of
      the radiation also stretch.
    - Generally, anything that has a (non-negligible) pressure
      will not behave in this particular way. Cosmologists
      therefore also say dark matter is "a pressureless fluid."
    - And, since I know it's confusing, let me remind you that
      a fluid isn't the same as a liquid, and gases can be
      fluids, so sometimes they may speak about "pressureless
      gas" instead.
    - In contrast to its name Dark Matter isn't dark. "Dark"
      suggests that it adsorbs light, but really it doesn't react
      with light at all. It would be better to call it
      _transparent_. Light just goes through.
    - And in return, Dark Matter just goes through all normal
      matter, including planet Earth and you and me. Dark Matter
      interacts even less often than the already elusive
      neutrinos.
    - Dark matter is what makes galaxies rotate faster and helps
      galactic structure formation to get started.
    - Dark Energy, too, is transparent rather than dark. But its
      name is even more misleading than Dark Matter because Dark
      Energy isn't energy either. Instead, if you divide it by
      Newton's constant, you get an energy density.
    - In contrast to Dark Matter, however, this energy-density
      does not dilute with the inverse volume. Instead, it
      doesn't dilute at all as the volume increases, at least not
      noticeably.
    - If the energy density remains entirely constant with the
      increase of volume, it's called the "cosmological
      constant." General types of Dark Energy can have a density
      that changes with time (or location), but we currently do
      not have any evidence that this is the case. The
      cosmological constant, for now, does just fine to explain
      observations.
    - Dark Energy is what makes the expansion of the universe
      speed up.
    - Are Dark Matter and Dark Energy the same?
    - Dark Matter and Dark Energy have distinctly different
      properties and cannot just be the same. At best they can
      both be different aspects of a common underlying theory.
      There are many theories for how this could happen, but to
      date we have no compelling evidence that this idea is
      correct

### intertial frames

      a neutral test particle and check it doesn't "move" around

### WEP - Weak Equivalence Principle
    - WEP:
        - gravitational mass = inertial mass.
        - `m_g = m_i`

    - WEP':
        - uniform acceleration = uniform external gravitational
          field.
        - For _massive_ objects and _external_ gravitation,
          a uniform external gravitational field is
          indistinguishable from uniform acceleration.
        - `a = g`
        - note: for "small" labs and short times

    - EEP:
        - Einstein Equivalence principle.
        - For _any_ object and _any_ force (EXCEPT internal
          gravitation), a uniform external gravitational field is
          indistinguishable from uniform acceleration.
        - the EEP can lead to several different gravitation
          theories (e.g. Brans-Dicke theory)

    - SEP:
        - Strong Equivalence principle.
        - For _any_ object and _any_ force, a uniform external
          gravitational field is indistinguishable from uniform
          acceleration.
        - the SEP leads only to Einsteins GR

### GR
    - coordinates versus components (difference!)
    - we started our vector story by seeing how COORDINATES
      transform and using that to see how vector COMPONENTS
      transform
    - but physics is interesting when things change. So we need
      to see how a derivative transforms
    - dont mix up [COORDINATES] and [VECTOR COMPONENTS]
        - coordinate: `X^ν`
            - you can't have a lower index of this
            - (ie a 'dual coordinate'? what does that mean)
        - component: `V^ν`
            - but you CAN have `V_ν` which is a dual vector
              component
    - random comment: a vector with 3 components is a tensor
      under rotations.
        - ie it transforms correctly.
        - ie vectors are perfectly good tensors under spatial
          rotations
    - a BOOST is manifestly a 4D thing: it combines time and
      space

### Tensors
    - two definitions!
    - A tensor is something that transforms like a tensor!
    - Tensors are multi-linear maps from the space of vectors and
      dual vectors into the REALS.
        - (what? in the end tensors evaluate as a number?)
        - `T(V, V, V, W, W) -> real number`
    - For tensors, we're always going to have EVERYTHING either
      primed or un-primed
    - rank 1 tensors (vectors) -> we can represent as column and
      row vectors
    - rank 2 tensors -> we can represent as 2D matrices
    - but as soon as you get 3 or more indices (rank 3 tensors)
      you can't write it as a matrix
    - remember: tensors are PHYSICAL objects.
    - But the lamdas `Λ` you see in transformation expressions
      are NOT tensors!
    - ie transformations `Λ` are (should be) invertible.
    - But don't know what it means to say a tensor is invertible.
    - remember: a transformation will be the only thing with one
      primed, and one un-primed index.

### GR components and basis vectors

### dual vectors: "rulers!"
    - how to get a map from a vector to its dual vector?
    - we use the metric
    - why do we need the dual vector?
    - we need it for the dot product...
    - Another point of view for duals:
        - The dual is intuitively the space of "_rulers_" (or
          measurement-instruments) for our vector space.
        - Its elements MEASURE vectors.
        - This is what makes the dual space and its relatives so
          important in Differential Geometry. This immediately
          motivates the study of the dual space.

        - This also helps intuitively explain some facts.
        - Eg. the fact that there is no canonical isomorphism
          between a vector space and its dual can then be seen as
          a consequence of RULERS needing scaling, and there is
          no canonical way to provide one scaling for space.
        - However, if we were to measure the measure-instruments,
          how could we proceed? Is there a canonical way to do
          so?
        - Well, if we want to measure our measures, why not
          measure them by how they act on what they are supposed
          to measure? We need no bases for that.
        - This justifies intuitively why there is a natural
          embedding of the space on its bidual. (Note, however,
          that this fails to justify why it is an isomorphism in
          the finite-dimensional case).

### Differential forms
    - the components of a form are completely ANTI-SYMMETRIC
    - `A_0 B_1 = - A_1 B_0`
    - However you're not guaranteed to get antisymmetric
      components if you naively _multiply_ two forms together (ie
      write out their components side-by-side)
    - $MY_IMAGES/physics/differential-forms-naive-multiply.jpg
    - so to multiply forms, we use the _wedge_ product
    - ie, the wedge product is defined that you explicitly get
      a form back when you multiply 2 forms together.


### Can light beams INTERACT/INTERFERE with each other
    - Summary:
        - interaction and interference are different things.
        - interaction: yes: photon-photon scattering can happen
          at very high energies
        - But normally we say light waves pass by each other
          unaffected (cos Maxwells EM equations are _linear_?)
    - but I was confused because of interference in the
      double-slit experiment. There you have beams interfering
      with each other??
    - but then I saw this:
        - One can see interference between two light beams, but
          _interference is not interaction_. Interference comes
          from the superposition of two beams collective
          wavefunctions, which when detected show the
          interference pattern from the way the photons' wave
          functions build up the macroscopic light beam.
        - _Superposition is not interaction_, so the beams can
          cross and continue on their way, if a detector is not
          introduced in the overlap.

    - two photons don't _interact_ with each other (except at
      incredibly high energies), so two light beams can pass
      through each other.

    - Two photons can _interfere_ with each other though, but
      that doesn't stop both photon beams from passing through
      each other. (Just like water waves from two directions
      exhibit interference when passing through each other).
      - Peter Shor

    - See also: HN Creating matter from light
      https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=28521970
        - Comment: In classical electromagnetism photons (light)
          do not interact, period. But in QM the
          photons can briefly fluctuate into electron + positron
          pairs which allow them to interact with each other. But
          the probability for interacting is very small AND you
          need enough energy to make the electron positron pair
          (for the Breit-Wheeler process), so it is very rare in
          practice.
        - I am not sure about the "time" the photons collide, but
          the interesting thing is that the _Breit-Wheeler_
          process is what determines the opacity of the universe
          - since high energy photons traveling through the
            universe can hit low energy photons from the cosmic
            microwave background and convert (disappearing) into
            an electron positron pair.
            - Reply
            - This is a good example of one of the errors that
              I often see in what is otherwise the best
              scientific writing, which is either forgetting to
              specify what a certain model says, or simply
              straight up forgetting/confusing that a certain
              model isn't reality.
            - Maxwell's equations say that light doesn't
              interact. Maxwell's equations are known to be wrong
              in that way. They're still a very good simplifying
              case that you can do very well with using, by all
              means, just like Newtonian physics in the right
              conditions, but they aren't the way reality works.
              Maxwell's equations also have no place to put
              a gravity term, yet gravity clearly affects light.
            - The one I probably see the most often is articles
              about black holes confidently speaking about "what
              goes on below the event horizon" from an Einstein
              relativity point of view, which is where you get
              all the singularities and ring singularities that
              lead to different universes somehow, etc, again
              either forgetting to point out or simply forgetting
              entirely that those are the specific predictions of
              Einstein relativity, which is known to be
              inadequate to describe the inside of a black hole.
              It is certainly fair to discuss that theory's
              predictions, and whatever really is happening in
              there, relativity will certain shine some sort of
              light on it, but it is a mistake to present it
              simply as "what happens on the inside". The model
              is known to be broken here.
